Cosmology — Big Bang, CMB & the ΛCDM Model

Deep dive into Cosmology — the observational foundations of the expanding universe (Hubble's law, the cosmic distance ladder, and the Hubble tension), the Friedmann-Lemaître-Robertson-Walker metric ds² = −dt² + a(t)² [dr²/(1−kr²) + r² dΩ²] and the Friedmann equations H² = (8πG/3)ρ − k/a² + Λ/3 and ä/a = −(4πG/3)(ρ + 3p) + Λ/3, the thermal history from the Planck epoch through grand unification, the electroweak phase transition, and quark-hadron transition, Big Bang nucleosynthesis (the abundances of ²H, ³He, ⁴He, ⁷Li as probes of baryon density), the cosmic microwave background (the recombination epoch, the surface of last scattering, the dipole anisotropy, and the Planck satellite measurements of the temperature and polarization power spectra), the dark matter evidence from rotation curves, gravitational lensing, and the Bullet Cluster, the dark energy from Type Ia supernovae (the Riess and Perlmutter discoveries), the cosmological constant problem (the 120 orders of magnitude discrepancy), cosmic inflation (the slow-roll conditions, the inflaton field, the generation of primordial perturbations, and the BICEP/Keck constraints on tensor modes), the ΛCDM concordance model parameters (Ω_m ≈ 0.315, Ω_Λ ≈ 0.685, H₀ ≈ 67.4 km/s/Mpc), and the growth of large-scale structure from the primordial power spectrum to the cosmic web.

"The most incomprehensible thing about the universe is that it is comprehensible."
— Albert Einstein
Part 1

🌌 The Expanding Universe

Cosmic Expansion
The FLRW Metric, Friedmann Equations, and Hubble's Law
The cosmological principle — the universe is homogeneous and isotropic on large scales — leads to the FLRW metric ds² = −dt² + a(t)² [dr²/(1−kr²) + r² (dθ² + sin²θ dφ²)], where a(t) is the scale factor and k = {−1, 0, +1} describes open, flat, or closed spatial geometry. The Friedmann equations follow from the Einstein field equations: H² = (ȧ/a)² = (8πG/3) ρ − k/a² + Λ/3 (first equation) and ä/a = −(4πG/3)(ρ + 3p) + Λ/3 (second/acceleration equation). The Hubble parameter H(t) = ȧ/a defines the expansion rate; its present value H₀ ≈ 67–74 km/s/Mpc is the Hubble constant. The Hubble law v = H₀ d (observed by Hubble in 1929) relates recessional velocity to distance. The deceleration parameter q₀ = −(ä a / ȧ²)_t₀. The critical density ρ_c = 3H²/(8πG) defines the density of a flat universe. The density parameters Ω_m = ρ_m/ρ_c, Ω_r = ρ_r/ρ_c, Ω_Λ = Λ/(3H²), and Ω_k = −k/(a²H²) satisfy Ω_m + Ω_r + Ω_Λ + Ω_k = 1. The redshift z is related to the scale factor by 1 + z = a₀/a. The comoving distance d_C = c ∫₀ᶻ dz/H(z). The proper distance d = a(t) d_C. The particle horizon d_H = c ∫₀ᵗ dt/a(t) limits causal contact. The cosmic distance ladder calibrates distances from parallax to Cepheids to Type Ia supernovae. The Hubble tension — the discrepancy between early-universe (CMB-based) H₀ ≈ 67.4 km/s/Mpc and late-universe (distance ladder) H₀ ≈ 73 km/s/Mpc — is a major open problem in cosmology.
The Friedmann equations govern the expansion history of the universe, linking the geometry, matter content, and dynamics through a single parameter — the scale factor a(t).
Part 2

🔥 The Hot Big Bang: Nucleosynthesis and the CMB

Thermal History
BBN, Recombination, the CMB Power Spectrum, and the Silk Damping Scale
Big Bang nucleosynthesis (BBN) occurred between t ≈ 1 s and t ≈ 3 min at T ≈ 10^9 K. The neutron-to-proton ratio n/p = exp(−Δm/kT) freezes out at ∼ 1/6, then neutrons decay (n → p + e⁻ + ν̄_e) until deuterium bottleneck at T ≈ 0.07 MeV, when D formation (p + n → D + γ) opens the path to ⁴He via D(p,γ)³He and D(D,n)³He reactions. The primordial ⁴He mass fraction Y_p ≈ 0.25 is determined by the baryon-to-photon ratio η = n_b/n_γ. Recombination at z ≈ 1100 (t ≈ 380,000 yr, T ≈ 3000 K) occurs when free electrons combine with protons to form neutral hydrogen: p + e⁻ → H + γ. The Saha equation for hydrogen ionization x_e = n_e/n_p satisfies x_e²/(1−x_e) = 1/(η n_γ) (m_e kT/2πℏ²)^{3/2} e^{−I_H/kT}. The visibility function peaks at z ≈ 1100 — the surface of last scattering. The CMB temperature today is T₀ = 2.725 K, with a perfect blackbody spectrum (COBE FIRAS measured the deviation < 10⁻⁵). The CMB anisotropies are expanded in spherical harmonics: ΔT/T = Σ_{ℓ,m} a_{ℓm} Y_{ℓm}(θ,φ). The angular power spectrum C_ℓ = ⟨|a_{ℓm}|²⟩ shows acoustic peaks from sound waves in the photon-baryon plasma before recombination. The first peak at ℓ ≈ 200 corresponds to the angular size of the sound horizon at last scattering, measuring the spatial curvature (Ω_k). The Sakharov oscillations — the compression and rarefaction of the plasma — produce alternating peaks. The Silk damping scale (photon diffusion) exponentially suppresses power at ℓ > 2000. The polarization E-modes and B-modes (from gravitational lensing and potentially primordial gravitational waves) provide additional probes. Sunyaev-Zel'dovich clusters and the integrated Sachs-Wolfe effect (ISW) probe the growth of structure and dark energy.
The CMB is the oldest light in the universe, encoding a snapshot of the universe at age 380,000 years — its anisotropies contain a wealth of cosmological parameters.
Part 3

🌑 Dark Matter and Dark Energy

Dark Components
Evidence for Dark Matter, the Cosmological Constant, and the Hubble Tension
Dark matter evidence spans multiple scales. On galactic scales, flat rotation curves (Rubin, Ford) require an extended dark halo with M/L >> 1. Gravitational lensing — both strong (Einstein rings, multiple images) and weak (statistical shear) — maps the total mass distribution. The Bullet Cluster provides direct evidence: the X-ray gas lags behind the collisionless dark matter (traced by weak lensing), separating baryonic from dark mass. The ΛCDM model uses cold dark matter (CDM) — non-relativistic, collisionless particles interacting only gravitationally. Candidates include WIMPs (weakly interacting massive particles at the electroweak scale predicted by supersymmetry), axions (from the Peccei-Quinn solution to the strong CP problem), and sterile neutrinos. Direct detection experiments (LZ, XENONnT, DarkSide) search for nuclear recoils from WIMP-nucleus scattering. Dark energy was discovered via Type Ia supernovae (Perlmutter 1998, Riess 1998): high-z SNe Ia are dimmer than expected in a matter-dominated universe, implying accelerated expansion (q₀ < 0). The simplest explanation is the cosmological constant Λ: a vacuum energy density ρ_Λ = Λ/(8πG). The equation of state w = p/ρ characterizes dark energy: w = −1 for a cosmological constant, w > −1 for quintessence (a scalar field slowly rolling down a potential). The cosmological constant problem: quantum field theory predicts a vacuum energy ∼ (10^19 GeV)^4 / (8π) ≈ 10^74 GeV^4, while the observed dark energy is ∼ 10^{−47} GeV^4 — a discrepancy of 120 orders of magnitude. The coincidence problem asks why Ω_m ∼ Ω_Λ at the present epoch when they scale so differently (ρ_m ∝ a⁻³, ρ_Λ ∝ const). Future probes (Euclid, Roman, Rubin LSST, DESI) will constrain w and its time evolution w(a) = w₀ + w_a(1−a).
Ordinary baryonic matter constitutes only ∼5% of the cosmic energy budget — the universe is dominated by dark matter (∼27%) and dark energy (∼68%), two components whose fundamental nature remains unknown.
Part 4

💥 Cosmic Inflation and the ΛCDM Concordance Model

Early Universe
The Inflationary Paradigm, Primordial Perturbations, and the Standard Model of Cosmology
Cosmic inflation (Guth 1981, Linde 1982, Albrecht-Steinhardt 1982) postulates a period of exponential expansion driven by a scalar field φ (the inflaton) with potential V(φ). The slow-roll conditions: ε = M_Pl² (V'/V)² / (16π) << 1 and η = M_Pl² V''/(8π V) << 1. During slow-roll, H² ≈ (8πG/3) V ≈ const, so a(t) ∝ exp(Ht). Inflation solves the horizon problem (the CMB is uniform across causally disconnected regions) and the flatness problem (Ω₀ is very close to 1). Quantum fluctuations in the inflaton field generate primordial curvature perturbations with nearly scale-invariant power spectrum P_ζ(k) = A_s (k/k_*) ^{n_s−1}. The scalar spectral index n_s ≈ 0.965 (Planck 2018) deviates from exact scale invariance, supporting inflation. Tensor modes (primordial gravitational waves) produce B-mode polarization in the CMB, parameterized by the tensor-to-scalar ratio r < 0.036 (BICEP/Keck 2021). The ΛCDM concordance model parameters from Planck 2018: H₀ = 67.4 ± 0.5 km/s/Mpc, Ω_m = 0.315 ± 0.007, Ω_Λ = 0.685 ± 0.007, Ω_b = 0.0493 ± 0.0003, n_s = 0.965 ± 0.004, σ₈ = 0.811 ± 0.006 (amplitude of matter fluctuations). The matter power spectrum P(k) grows from the primordial spectrum via the transfer function T(k) (which encodes the matter-radiation equality scale k_eq). Baryon acoustic oscillations (BAO) in the galaxy distribution provide a standard ruler at the sound horizon scale ∼ 150 Mpc. The cosmic web of voids, filaments, and clusters emerges from gravitational collapse of the primordial density field, described by the Zel'dovich approximation and N-body simulations. The end of the dark ages (z ∼ 6–15) witnessed the formation of the first stars (Population III) and galaxies during the Epoch of Reionization.
Inflation provides a causal mechanism for the origin of structure in the universe, generating the primordial seeds for all cosmic structures from quantum fluctuations stretched to cosmological scales.

🎯 Practice

← Back to Physics Map

Structure Formation and the Growth of Cosmic Perturbations

The transition from the nearly uniform primordial density field to the cosmic web of galaxies, clusters, and voids is governed by gravitational instability. In the linear regime, density perturbations δ ≡ δρ/ρ grow according to the Meszaros equation for a universe containing radiation and matter. During radiation domination, sub-horizon perturbations in the dark matter component grow only logarithmically with the scale factor (δ ∝ ln a) because radiation pressure prevents gravitational collapse. After matter-radiation equality (z_eq ≈ 3400), the growth enters the matter-dominated regime where δ ∝ a for modes well inside the horizon. The linear growth factor D(a) quantifies this evolution and is normalized to unity today. In a ΛCDM universe, the growth factor is suppressed at late times by dark energy, leading to the well-known growth index parameterization f ≡ d ln D / d ln a ≈ Ω_m(a)^γ with γ ≈ 0.55.

The matter power spectrum P(k) = ⟨|δ_k|²⟩ encodes the statistical distribution of density fluctuations as a function of comoving wavenumber k. It is shaped by the primordial power spectrum P_prim(k) ∝ kn_s multiplied by the transfer function T(k)². The transfer function encodes the physical processes that modify perturbations between horizon entry and the present: radiation driving, Silk damping, and the baryon acoustic oscillations. The characteristic scale k_eq = 0.01 Mpc⁻¹ marks the horizon size at matter-radiation equality; modes with k ≫ k_eq entered the horizon during radiation domination and suffer suppression relative to larger modes. The turnover in P(k) at k ≈ k_eq is a direct probe of the matter density Ω_m h². Baryon acoustic oscillations imprint a series of peaks and troughs in P(k) with a characteristic spacing Δk ≈ π / r_s, where r_s ≈ 147 Mpc is the sound horizon at the drag epoch. These features serve as a standard ruler in galaxy surveys, enabling precise measurements of the angular diameter distance D_A(z) and the Hubble parameter H(z) through the Alcock-Paczynski test.

Nonlinear structure formation proceeds through hierarchical clustering: small-scale perturbations collapse first to form halos, which then merge into larger structures. The Press-Schechter formalism and its extensions (Sheth-Tormen, Tinker) provide analytic predictions for the halo mass function dn/dM, the comoving number density of halos per unit mass. The halo bias b(M) relates the halo overdensity to the matter overdensity and is scale-dependent on large scales due to primordial non-Gaussianity. N-body simulations remain the primary tool for modeling the fully nonlinear regime, with modern simulations (e.g., Millennium, IllustrisTNG, AbacusSummit) achieving dynamic ranges sufficient to resolve subhalos within cluster-scale halos. The halo model decomposes the matter power spectrum into one-halo (internal structure) and two-halo (clustering) terms, providing a framework for interpreting galaxy-galaxy lensing and cluster counts. Redshift-space distortions (RSD) introduce anisotropies in the observed galaxy power spectrum due to peculiar velocities; the Kaiser formula describes the linear regime (P_g(k, μ) = (b + f μ²)² P_m(k)), while the Finger-of-God effect from virialized motions suppresses power on small scales along the line of sight.

Reionization and the 21-cm Line as a Cosmological Probe

The Epoch of Reionization (EoR) marks the transition of the intergalactic medium (IGM) from neutral to highly ionized, driven by ultraviolet photons from the first stars and galaxies. The process is patchy and extended, typically spanning z ≈ 6–10, with the midpoint constrained by Planck optical depth measurements to z_re ≈ 7.7 ± 0.7. The Gunn-Peterson trough in quasar spectra at z > 6 confirms that the IGM was significantly neutral at these redshifts. The 21-cm hyperfine transition of neutral hydrogen (rest frequency 1420.4 MHz) provides a unique tomographic probe of the EoR. The differential brightness temperature relative to the CMB is δT_b ≈ 27 x_HI (1 + δ) (1 - T_CMB/T_S) √((1+z)/10) (0.15/Ω_m h²) mK, where x_HI is the neutral fraction, δ the matter overdensity, and T_S the spin temperature. During the cosmic dawn (z ≈ 15–30), Lyman-α photons from the first stars couple T_S to the gas kinetic temperature T_K via the Wouthuysen-Field effect, driving T_S below T_CMB and producing a strong absorption signal against the CMB. The EDGES experiment reported an anomalously deep absorption trough at z ≈ 17, suggesting either colder gas or excess radio background, though this remains controversial.

As X-ray heating from early X-ray binaries and miniquasars raises T_K above T_CMB, the 21-cm signal transitions to emission. The morphology of ionized bubbles during reionization imprints characteristic fluctuations in the 21-cm power spectrum. On large scales (k < 0.1 Mpc⁻¹), the power spectrum is dominated by the clustering of ionizing sources and the bubble size distribution; on small scales, it reflects the density field within neutral regions. The evolution of the 21-cm power spectrum with redshift — particularly the peak amplitude and the turnover scale — encodes the reionization history, the typical halo mass of ionizing sources, and the mean free path of ionizing photons. Current interferometers (HERA, LOFAR, MWA, SKA-Low) target the statistical detection of the 21-cm power spectrum. Foreground removal is the primary challenge: galactic synchrotron and extragalactic point sources exceed the cosmological signal by 4–5 orders of magnitude. The smooth frequency spectrum of foregrounds allows separation via polynomial fitting or delay-spectrum techniques, but instrumental chromaticity (the "wedge") contaminates modes that would otherwise be clean. The global (sky-averaged) 21-cm signal, measured by single-dipole experiments (SARAS, LEDA, PRIZM), provides complementary constraints on the timing of cosmic dawn and reionization, though systematic calibration errors remain a significant limitation.

Worked Problem: Growth Factor in ΛCDM
Calculate the linear growth factor D(a) at scale factor a = 0.5 in a flat ΛCDM universe with Ω_m,0 = 0.315 and Ω_Λ,0 = 0.685, normalized such that D(a=1) = 1. Use the integral form D(a) = (5 Ω_m,0 / 2) H(a) ∫0^a da' / (a' H(a'))³ where H(a) = H0 √(Ω_m,0 a⁻³ + Ω_Λ,0).
First compute H(a)/H0 at a = 0.5: H(0.5)/H0 = √(0.315 × 0.5⁻³ + 0.685) = √(0.315 × 8 + 0.685) = √(2.52 + 0.685) = √3.205 ≈ 1.790. The integral I(a) = ∫0^a da' / (a' H(a')/H0)³. Change variable to x = a'3/2 or evaluate numerically. For a = 0.5, the integral from 0 to 0.5 of da' / (a'³ (Ω_m,0 a'⁻³ + Ω_Λ,0)3/2) = ∫00.5 da' / (Ω_m,0 + Ω_Λ,0 a'³)3/2. Numerical integration yields I(0.5) ≈ 0.5 / (0.315)3/2 × (correction for Λ) ≈ 0.5 / 0.176 × 0.85 ≈ 2.41. More precisely, using the hypergeometric form or standard numerical integration: I(0.5) ≈ 2.38. Then D(0.5) = (5 × 0.315 / 2) × 1.790 × 2.38 ≈ 0.7875 × 1.790 × 2.38 ≈ 3.35. Normalize by D(1): compute D(1) similarly. H(1)/H0 = 1. I(1) = ∫01 da' / (Ω_m,0 + Ω_Λ,0 a'³)3/2 ≈ 3.25. D(1) = 0.7875 × 1 × 3.25 ≈ 2.56. Therefore D(0.5) = 3.35 / 2.56 ≈ 1.31. Wait — D(a) should be < 1 for a < 1. The normalization convention D(a=1)=1 means we divide by D(1). The integral form gives the unnormalized growth factor. The correct normalized D(0.5) = [H(0.5) I(0.5)] / [H(1) I(1)] = (1.790 × 2.38) / (1 × 3.25) ≈ 4.26 / 3.25 ≈ 1.31. This is > 1, which is wrong. The error: the integral lower limit should be handled carefully. The standard approximation D(a) ≈ a for a ≪ 1 in matter domination. At a=0.5, Ω_m(a) = 0.315×8 / 3.205 ≈ 0.786. The growth factor from a=0.5 to a=1 is D(1)/D(0.5) = exp(∫0.51 f(a) d ln a). Using f ≈ Ω_m0.55: at a=0.5, f≈0.7860.55≈0.86; at a=1, f≈0.3150.55≈0.51. Average f≈0.68. Δln a = ln 2 ≈ 0.693. Growth ratio ≈ exp(0.68×0.693) ≈ exp(0.47) ≈ 1.60. So D(0.5) ≈ 1/1.60 ≈ 0.625. The integral formula requires the growing mode solution normalized to a at early times. The correct normalized D(0.5) ≈ 0.62–0.63. The exact value from cosmology codes (CAMB/CLASS) for these parameters is D(0.5) ≈ 0.623.
Exam Strategy: Growth Factor Approximations
Memorize the growth index γ ≈ 0.55 for ΛCDM and the approximation f ≈ Ω_m(a)^γ. For quick estimates, use D(a) ≈ a during matter domination (a ≪ 1) and D(a) ≈ constant during Λ domination. The growth suppression factor g = D(a=1) / a=1 in a ΛCDM universe relative to Einstein-de Sitter is g ≈ 0.77 for Ω_m,0 = 0.3. Questions often ask for the ratio of growth factors at two redshifts; use the integral of f d ln a rather than the full integral formula. Know that σ8(z) = σ8,0 × D(z)/D(0) and that cluster counts are exponentially sensitive to σ8.

Primordial Non-Gaussianity and Inflationary Model Discrimination

While the primordial curvature perturbation ζ is predominantly Gaussian, inflationary models generically predict small non-Gaussian corrections characterized by the bispectrum B_ζ(k₁, k₂, k₃) = ⟨ζk₁ ζk₂ ζk₃⟩. The local ansatz parametrizes the leading-order non-Gaussianity as ζ(x) = ζ_G(x) + (3/5) f_NL (ζ_G(x)² - ⟨ζ_G²⟩), where ζ_G is a Gaussian field. The factor 3/5 is a historical convention from the Sachs-Wolfe effect. The bispectrum in the squeezed limit (k₁ ≪ k₂ ≈ k₃) is B_ζ(k₁, k₂, k₃) → (6/5) f_NL P_ζ(k₁) P_ζ(k₂). Single-field slow-roll inflation predicts f_NL^local = (5/12)(1 - n_s) ≈ 0.015, which is unobservably small. A detection of |f_NL| ≳ 1 would rule out the simplest single-field models and point to multi-field dynamics (curvaton, modulated reheating), non-Bunch-Davies initial states, or higher-derivative operators (DBI inflation, ghost inflation). The Planck 2018 constraints are f_NL^local = -0.9 ± 5.1, f_NL^equil = -26 ± 47, f_NL^ortho = -38 ± 24 (68% CL), consistent with Gaussian initial conditions.

The shape of the bispectrum distinguishes inflationary mechanisms. Equilateral non-Gaussianity (peaked at k₁ = k₂ = k₃) arises from higher-derivative kinetic terms that reduce the sound speed c_s < 1, with f_NL^equil ∝ 1/c_s². Orthogonal non-Gaussianity is a linear combination designed to be uncorrelated with local and equilateral templates, capturing features like those from non-trivial initial states. The trispectrum parameter τ_NL and the collapse fraction of primordial black holes provide additional constraints. On large scales, local non-Gaussianity induces a scale-dependent halo bias Δb(k) = 2 f_NL (b - 1) δ_c Ω_m H0² / (k² T(k) D(z)), where δ_c ≈ 1.686 is the critical collapse density. This k⁻² signature is a powerful probe: galaxy surveys (DESI, Euclid, Roman, SPHEREx) target σ(f_NL) ≈ 1–2. The Maldacena consistency relation for single-field inflation states that in the squeezed limit, the bispectrum is fixed by the spectral index: limk₁→0 B_ζ(k₁, k₂, k₃) = -(n_s - 1) P_ζ(k₁) P_ζ(k₂). This is a direct consequence of diffeomorphism invariance and the adiabatic nature of the perturbations. Violations would signal multiple fields or non-adiabatic modes. The CMB bispectrum is measured via optimal estimators using needlet or modal expansions; the primary limitation is the cosmic variance of the Gaussian part and the lensing-induced bispectrum, which mimics f_NL^local ≈ 0.5–1.

The Hubble Tension and Early Dark Energy

The Hubble tension refers to the 4–6σ discrepancy between the local distance-ladder measurement of H0 (SH0ES: 73.04 ± 1.04 km/s/Mpc from Cepheids and SNe Ia) and the value inferred from the CMB under ΛCDM (Planck: 67.4 ± 0.5 km/s/Mpc). The tension persists across multiple independent early-universe probes (BAO+BBN, strong lensing time delays, TRGB, Mira variables) and late-universe probes, suggesting either unaccounted systematics or new physics. The CMB-inferred H0 is degenerate with the sound horizon at recombination r_s = ∫0t_* c_s dt / a. Reducing r_s while keeping the angular scale θ_* = r_s / D_A(z_*) fixed requires a higher H0. Early dark energy (EDE) models introduce a scalar field that contributes a fraction f_EDE ≈ 0.1 of the energy density around matter-radiation equality (z ~ 3000–5000), then dilutes rapidly (w ≈ 1). This reduces r_s by ~7% and raises the CMB-inferred H0 to ~71 km/s/Mpc, alleviating the tension. However, EDE worsens the fit to the CMB damping tail and the matter power spectrum amplitude (S8 tension), and requires fine-tuned initial conditions and potential parameters.

Alternative resolutions include: (1) Late-time modifications (phantom dark energy w < -1, interacting dark energy) which are constrained by BAO and SNe Ia; (2) New relativistic species (ΔN_eff > 0) which increase the expansion rate before recombination but are bounded by BBN and CMB damping tail; (3) A primordial magnetic field or inhomogeneous recombination altering the sound horizon; (4) Systematic errors in the distance ladder (Cepheid metallicity, crowding, Gaia parallax zero-point). The "sound horizon problem" reframes the tension: local measurements anchor the late-time expansion, while CMB anchors the early-universe sound horizon. Independent measurements of r_s from BAO at z > 2 (e.g., Lyman-α forest) and from the CMB damping tail provide cross-checks. The SH0ES team's use of the TRGB (Tip of the Red Giant Branch) as an alternative to Cepheids yields H0 = 69.8 ± 1.9 km/s/Mpc, intermediate between the two extremes. Future gravitational-wave standard sirens (LIGO/Virgo/KAGRA, LISA, Einstein Telescope) will provide a completely independent H0 measurement via the luminosity distance–redshift relation of binary neutron star mergers, with a target precision of 1–2% within the next decade.

Gravitational Waves as Cosmological Probes

Gravitational waves (GWs) offer a unique window into cosmology because their amplitude and frequency evolution directly encode the luminosity distance D_L(z) without a cosmic distance ladder. For a compact binary inspiral, the observed strain amplitude h ∝ M_c5/3 f2/3 / D_L, where M_c = (m₁ m₂)3/5 / (m₁ + m₂)1/5 is the chirp mass. The frequency evolution df/dt ∝ M_c5/3 f11/3 breaks the mass–distance degeneracy, yielding D_L directly. The redshift is obtained from an electromagnetic counterpart (for binary neutron stars) or statistically from galaxy catalogs (for binary black holes). The Hubble constant measurement from GW170817 (binary neutron star with GRB 170817A and kilonova AT2017gfo) gave H0 = 70.0+12.0-8.0 km/s/Mpc. With ~50 bright sirens, the precision reaches ~2%. Third-generation detectors (Einstein Telescope, Cosmic Explorer) will detect binary neutron stars to z ~ 2–3, enabling a full H(z) reconstruction.

The stochastic gravitational-wave background (SGWB) from unresolved astrophysical sources (compact binaries) and cosmological sources (inflation, cosmic strings, phase transitions) is characterized by the energy density spectrum Ω_GW(f) = (1/ρ_c) dρ_GW/d ln f. Inflationary tensor modes produce a nearly scale-invariant Ω_GW ∝ fn_t with n_t = -r/8 (consistency relation). Current pulsar timing arrays (NANOGrav, EPTA, PPTA, IPTA) probe nHz frequencies and have reported evidence for a stochastic process consistent with a population of supermassive black hole binaries, though a cosmological component (e.g., cosmic strings with tension Gμ ~ 10⁻¹¹) is not excluded. LISA will probe mHz frequencies, targeting the SGWB from a first-order electroweak phase transition (peak frequency ~ mHz, amplitude Ω_GW ~ 10⁻¹²–10⁻¹⁰) and from cosmic strings. The anisotropies in the SGWB (angular power spectrum C_ℓ) trace the large-scale structure and provide a new probe of bias and primordial non-Gaussianity. GW lensing — magnification, time delays, and diffraction — will become detectable with third-generation detectors, enabling GW cosmography independent of electromagnetic counterparts. The "dark siren" method uses the galaxy catalog cross-correlation to infer the redshift distribution of binary black hole mergers, circumventing the need for counterparts. The primary systematics are the binary population model (mass distribution, merger rate evolution) and peculiar velocity contamination at low redshift.

  • Confusing the growth factor D(a) with the growth rate f(a); D(a) is the integral of f d ln a, not f itself.
  • Assuming the 21-cm signal is always in absorption; it transitions from absorption to emission as X-ray heating raises the gas temperature above the CMB temperature.
  • Treating f_NL as a single number without specifying the shape (local, equilateral, orthogonal); constraints and physical implications differ drastically.
  • Attributing the Hubble tension solely to early dark energy without addressing the concomitant S8 tension and CMB damping tail fit.
  • Neglecting that standard sirens measure luminosity distance D_L, not angular diameter distance D_A; the relation D_L = (1+z)² D_A holds only in standard cosmology.
  • Overlooking the foreground wedge in 21-cm interferometry; modes inside the wedge are contaminated by spectrally smooth foregrounds leaking due to instrumental chromaticity.
  • Using the Press-Schechter mass function without the Sheth-Tormen or Tinker corrections for ellipsoidal collapse, which significantly improves agreement with simulations.

Baryon Acoustic Oscillations as a Standard Ruler

Baryon acoustic oscillations (BAO) arise from sound waves that propagated in the photon–baryon fluid before recombination. The comoving sound horizon at the drag epoch, rs, imprints a preferred scale in the late‑time clustering of galaxies and in the Lyman‑α forest. In configuration space this appears as a peak in the two‑point correlation function ξ(r) at r ≈ rs; in Fourier space it produces a series of damped oscillations in the power spectrum P(k). Because rs is calibrated by the CMB (Planck 2018 gives rs = 147.09 ± 0.26 Mpc), BAO provides a geometric standard ruler: measuring the angular diameter distance DA(z) and the Hubble parameter H(z) through the transverse and radial clustering scales.

The observable is the dilation parameter α(z) = [DV(z)/rs] / [DV,fid(z)/rs,fid], where DV(z) = [z DA²(z) c/H(z)]1/3 is the volume‑averaged distance. A detection of α ≠ 1 signals a deviation from the fiducial cosmology. Modern surveys (BOSS, eBOSS, DESI, Euclid) fit the full anisotropic correlation function ξ(s⊥, s∥) or the multipoles Pℓ(k) to extract DA(z) and H(z) separately, breaking the degeneracy inherent in the isotropic DV measurement.

Systematic effects include non‑linear damping of the BAO peak, which is modeled by a Gaussian smoothing of the linear power spectrum with a scale Σnl ≈ 5–10 Mpc/h at z ≈ 0.5. Reconstruction techniques (standard or iterative) partially reverse this damping by displacing galaxies according to the estimated density field, sharpening the peak and improving distance precision by 30–50 %. Redshift‑space distortions (RSD) couple the BAO signal to the growth rate fσ₈; joint BAO+RSD analyses therefore constrain both geometry and growth simultaneously.

Cross‑correlations between different tracers (e.g., galaxies and quasars, or galaxies and the Lyman‑α forest) reduce sample variance and provide internal consistency checks. The Lyman‑α forest BAO at z ≈ 2.3 measures the Hubble parameter H(z) with ∼2 % precision, complementing low‑z galaxy BAO. Future 21‑cm intensity mapping (HIRAX, CHIME, SKA) aims to map BAO over 0.5 < z < 2.5 with high number density, offering a powerful probe of the expansion history and potential deviations from ΛCDM.

Weak Gravitational Lensing and Cosmic Shear

Weak gravitational lensing distorts the shapes of background galaxies by the tidal gravitational field of intervening large‑scale structure. The observable is the complex shear γ = γ₁ + iγ₂, which is related to the projected gravitational potential ψ via the lensing kernel. In the flat‑sky limit, the shear field can be decomposed into E‑mode (gradient) and B‑mode (curl) components; in standard cosmology only E‑modes are generated by scalar density perturbations, while B‑modes arise from systematics, intrinsic alignments, or primordial gravitational waves.

The two‑point statistics of the shear field are the cosmic shear correlation functions ξ+(θ) and ξ−(θ), or equivalently the convergence power spectrum Cℓκκ. Under the Limber approximation, Cℓκκ = ∫ dz (W(z)/χ(z))² Pδ(k = ℓ/χ(z), z), where W(z) = (3/2) Ωm H₀² (1+z) χ(z) ∫z∞ dz' n(z') (χ(z')−χ(z))/χ(z') is the lensing efficiency kernel, χ(z) the comoving distance, and n(z) the normalized source redshift distribution. The amplitude of Cℓ scales roughly as σ₈² Ωm1.5, making cosmic shear a sensitive probe of the σ₈–Ωm degeneracy.

Key systematics include shape measurement bias (multiplicative m and additive c), photometric redshift uncertainties that broaden n(z), intrinsic alignments (IA) of galaxies with the local tidal field, and baryonic feedback that suppresses small‑scale power. Modern analyses (DES Y3, KiDS‑1000, HSC Y3) marginalize over IA using the nonlinear alignment model (NLA) or the tidal alignment model (TATT), and incorporate baryonic corrections from hydrodynamical simulations (e.g., OWLS, BAHAMAS). The combination of cosmic shear with galaxy clustering (3×2pt analysis) breaks parameter degeneracies and yields constraints on S₈ = σ₈(Ωm/0.3)0.5 at the 2–3 % level.

Future Stage‑IV surveys (LSST, Euclid, Roman) will increase the effective source density to >30 galaxies/arcmin² and extend the redshift range to z ∼ 2, enabling tomographic binning in 10–20 redshift slices. This will allow a percent‑level measurement of the dark energy equation of state w(a) = w₀ + wₐ(1−a) and a test of modified gravity through the gravitational slip parameter η = Ψ/Φ and the effective Newton constant μ = Geff/G.

Examiner's trap: Misidentifying the BAO scale in correlation function
A galaxy survey measures the two‑point correlation function ξ(r) and finds a prominent peak at 105 h⁻¹ Mpc. The sound horizon at the drag epoch from Planck is rs = 147 Mpc. Assuming a fiducial cosmology with rs,fid = 147 Mpc, compute the dilation parameter α and state what α < 1 implies about the true distance scale relative to the fiducial model.
The dilation parameter α is defined as the ratio of the observed BAO scale to the true sound horizon, α = rpeak,obs / rs. Here rpeak,obs = 105 h⁻¹ Mpc and rs = 147 Mpc. Since the fiducial sound horizon equals the true value, α = 105 / 147 ≈ 0.714. An α < 1 means the observed peak occurs at a smaller comoving separation than expected; equivalently, the true volume‑averaged distance DV(z) is smaller than the fiducial DV,fid(z) by the same factor. This would indicate a higher Hubble parameter H(z) or a smaller angular diameter distance DA(z) than in the fiducial ΛCDM model.
Exam‑day strategy
Memorize the definition α = (DV/rs) / (DV,fid/rs,fid) and practice converting an observed BAO peak position into constraints on H(z) and DA(z). Remember that reconstruction sharpens the peak but does not shift its mean position; the shift is a genuine cosmological signal.

CMB Polarization and Primordial B‑Modes

Thomson scattering at recombination generates linear polarization in the CMB. The polarization field is described by the Stokes parameters Q and U, which can be decomposed into scalar E‑modes and pseudoscalar B‑modes on the sphere. Scalar density perturbations produce only E‑modes; tensor perturbations (primordial gravitational waves) generate both E‑ and B‑modes. The B‑mode power spectrum CℓBB at large angular scales (ℓ ≲ 100) is therefore a direct probe of the tensor‑to‑scalar ratio r = At/As.

The amplitude of the primordial B‑mode signal peaks at the recombination bump (ℓ ≈ 80) and the reionization bump (ℓ ≈ 5–10). For r = 0.01, the recombination bump reaches ≈ 0.1 μK². Current upper limits from the BICEP/Keck Array combined with Planck give r < 0.032 at 95 % CL (BK18+Planck 2018). The dominant foregrounds are polarized thermal dust emission (spectral index βd ≈ 1.5–1.6) and synchrotron radiation (βs ≈ −3). Multi‑frequency component separation (parametric fitting or blind methods such as NILC) is essential; residual foreground bias must be kept below the target r sensitivity.

Gravitational lensing of E‑modes by large‑scale structure converts a fraction of the E‑mode power into B‑modes, producing a lensing B‑mode spectrum that peaks at ℓ ≈ 1000 with amplitude ≈ 5 μK². This lensing B‑mode is a guaranteed signal and has been measured by SPTpol, POLARBEAR, and ACTPol. Delensing — using external tracers (CIB, galaxy surveys) or internal CMB minimum‑variance estimators — can suppress the lensing B‑mode by 50–80 %, improving the reach on r by a factor of ∼2.

Future experiments (Simons Observatory, CMB‑S4, LiteBIRD) aim for σ(r) ≈ 0.001–0.002. Achieving this requires control of instrumental systematics (beam mismatch, polarization angle calibration, scan strategy) at the sub‑μK level, and a thorough understanding of polarized foreground complexity (spatially varying spectral indices, decorrelation). A detection of r > 0.001 would strongly favor large‑field inflationary models (e.g., chaotic inflation) and provide a direct measurement of the inflationary energy scale V1/4 ≈ 2×10¹⁶ GeV (r/0.01)1/4.

Neutrino Cosmology: Masses and Effective Number of Species

Relic neutrinos decouple at T ≈ 1 MeV and contribute to the radiation energy density in the early universe. The effective number of neutrino species Neff parameterizes any deviation from the standard model prediction Neff = 3.044 (including finite‑temperature QED corrections). Planck 2018 constrains Neff = 2.99 ± 0.17 (68 % CL), consistent with three light neutrino families. Extra relativistic degrees of freedom (e.g., sterile neutrinos, dark radiation, axions) would increase Neff and shift the CMB damping tail and the BAO scale.

Neutrino masses Σmν affect cosmology primarily through the suppression of small‑scale matter power. Massive neutrinos free‑stream out of overdensities below their free‑streaming scale kfs ≈ 0.018 (mν/1 eV)¹/² Ωm¹/² h Mpc⁻¹, reducing the linear power spectrum by ≈ 8 fν where fν = Ων/Ωm ≈ Σmν/(93.14 Ωm h² eV). Current combined CMB+BAO+full‑shape galaxy clustering limits give Σmν < 0.12 eV (95 % CL) for the minimal ΛCDM+Σmν model. The lower bound from oscillation experiments is Σmν > 0.06 eV (normal ordering) or > 0.10 eV (inverted ordering).

Degeneracies exist between Σmν and other parameters: increasing Σmν can be partially compensated by raising σ₈ or lowering H₀. The CMB lensing power spectrum breaks the Σmν–σ₈ degeneracy because lensing probes the late‑time matter distribution directly. Future surveys (CMB‑S4, DESI, Euclid, LSST) target σ(Σmν) ≈ 0.02 eV, sufficient to distinguish the mass ordering at >3σ if the minimal mass scenario holds.

Neutrino self‑interactions or non‑standard neutrino properties (e.g., secret interactions, decay) can alter the phase shift of the BAO acoustic peaks and the CMB damping tail. These effects are constrained by the combination of CMB temperature/polarization spectra and BAO measurements, providing a unique laboratory for particle physics beyond the Standard Model.

Dark Energy Equation of State and Modified Gravity Tests

The accelerated expansion can be described by a dark energy component with equation of state w(a) = p/ρ. The Chevallier‑Polarski‑Linder (CPL) parametrization w(a) = w₀ + wₐ(1−a) is widely used; w₀ = −1, wₐ = 0 recovers the cosmological constant. Current constraints from Planck+BAO+SNe Ia give w₀ = −1.03 ± 0.03, wₐ = 0.0 ± 0.3 (68 % CL). The figure of merit (FoM) = 1/√det Cov(w₀,wₐ) quantifies the constraining power; Stage‑IV surveys aim for FoM > 1000.

Modified gravity theories alter the Poisson equation and the relationship between the metric potentials Ψ and Φ. In the quasi‑static limit on sub‑horizon scales, the effective Newton constant μ(k,a) = Geff/G and the gravitational slip η(k,a) = Ψ/Φ parameterize deviations. For Horndeski scalar‑tensor theories, μ and η are scale‑dependent functions of the scalar field kinetic term and braiding parameter. The combination of redshift‑space distortions (measuring fσ₈) and weak lensing (measuring Σ = (μ+η)/2) can separate μ and η.

Screening mechanisms (chameleon, symmetron, Vainshtein) restore General Relativity in high‑density environments, making solar‑system tests compatible with cosmological deviations. On linear scales, the effective field theory of dark energy (EFTofDE) provides a unified description with a small set of time‑dependent functions (αK, αB, αM, αT). The speed of gravitational waves cT = 1 + αT is constrained to |cT−1| < 10⁻¹⁵ by the binary neutron star event GW170817, forcing αT ≈ 0 and eliminating a large class of scalar‑tensor models.

Future 3×2pt analyses (galaxy clustering, galaxy‑galaxy lensing, cosmic shear) combined with CMB lensing and BAO will test the consistency of the growth history with the expansion history. A detection of μ ≠ 1 or η ≠ 1 at the few‑percent level would indicate physics beyond ΛCDM, while consistency would tighten the allowed parameter space for dark energy and modified gravity models.

  • Confusing the BAO dilation parameter α with the Alcock‑Paczynski parameter F = (1+z)DAH/c; they are related but distinct observables.
  • Assuming weak lensing B‑modes are solely due to systematics; lensing of E‑modes produces a physical B‑mode that must be modeled or delensed.
  • Treating the tensor‑to‑scalar ratio r as a free parameter without imposing the consistency relation nt = −r/8 for single‑field slow‑roll inflation.
  • Neglecting the neutrino free‑streaming scale when interpreting small‑scale matter power suppression; baryonic feedback mimics a similar effect.
  • Using the CPL parametrization outside its validity range (a → 0) where w(a) can diverge; alternative parametrizations (e.g., early dark energy) may be required.
  • Overlooking that GW170817 forces the tensor speed αT ≈ 0, which eliminates many Horndeski models that predict a varying gravitational wave speed.
  • Remember to cross‑check the sound horizon inferred from BAO with the CMB damping tail to avoid biased distance measurements.

Primordial Black Holes as Dark Matter Candidates

Primordial black holes (PBHs) form from the collapse of large density fluctuations in the early radiation‑dominated era, typically when a region re‑enters the horizon with an overdensity exceeding a critical threshold δc ≈ 0.3–0.5. The mass of a PBH is roughly the horizon mass at formation, M ≈ 10⁵ (t/1 s) M⊙, so PBHs spanning 10⁻¹⁸ M⊙ to 10⁵ M⊙ can be produced depending on the epoch of the fluctuation. If the primordial power spectrum has a pronounced peak on small scales — for instance from a brief period of ultra‑slow‑roll inflation or a phase transition — the resulting PBH abundance can be significant. Current constraints combine microlensing (MACHO, EROS, OGLE), accretion‑induced CMB anisotropies, dynamical heating of dwarf galaxies, and gravitational‑wave merger rates from LIGO/Virgo. For masses around 10⁻¹⁶–10⁻¹⁴ M⊙ (asteroid‑mass window) the microlensing limits are weakest, allowing PBHs to constitute up to ~10 % of the dark matter density. In the 10–100 M⊙ range, LIGO binary‑black‑hole mergers are compatible with a sub‑dominant PBH fraction (fPBH ≲ 0.01) but do not require it. Evaporation via Hawking radiation removes PBHs lighter than ~10¹⁵ g (≈10⁻¹⁸ M⊙) well before today, while heavier PBHs survive and behave as cold, collisionless dark matter. A key discriminant is the Poisson clustering of PBHs, which enhances small‑scale structure and can be probed by future 21‑cm intensity mapping and strong‑lensing flux‑ratio anomalies. The PBH scenario also predicts a stochastic gravitational‑wave background from second‑order scalar perturbations at formation, offering a target for pulsar‑timing arrays and space‑based interferometers such as LISA.

Cosmic Strings and Topological Defects in the CMB

Cosmic strings are one‑dimensional topological defects that can arise when a U(1) symmetry is spontaneously broken during a phase transition in the early universe, for example at the Grand Unification scale η ~ 10¹⁶ GeV. Their tension is μ ≈ η², and the dimensionless parameter Gμ/c² quantifies their gravitational effect. For Gμ/c² ≳ 10⁻⁷, strings produce observable signatures: they generate line‑like discontinuities in the CMB temperature (the Kaiser‑Stebbins effect) and source vector and tensor perturbations that contribute to the B‑mode polarization power spectrum. Unlike inflationary tensor modes, the string‑induced B‑mode spectrum is nearly scale‑invariant at low multipoles and peaks around ℓ ≈ 500–1000. Planck temperature and polarization data constrain Gμ/c² < 1.3 × 10⁻⁷ (95 % CL) assuming a standard Nambu‑Goto string network. Superconducting strings carrying currents can emit high‑energy particles and produce distinctive radio and gamma‑ray bursts, providing complementary probes. Cosmic superstrings from brane inflation have a spectrum of tensions and intercommutation probabilities, leading to a richer phenomenology including cusps and kinks that generate gravitational‑wave bursts detectable by LIGO/Virgo and future detectors. The non‑Gaussian signature of string‑induced temperature steps — sharp edges with a characteristic amplitude ΔT/T ≈ 8πGμ — can be searched for with wavelet or edge‑detection algorithms on high‑resolution CMB maps. Current limits already exclude the simplest GUT‑scale string models, but a network with Gμ/c² ~ 10⁻⁹–10⁻⁸ remains viable and would be within reach of CMB‑S4 and next‑generation gravitational‑wave observatories.

Worked Example: Estimating the CMB Temperature Step from a Cosmic String
A straight cosmic string with tension Gμ/c² = 5 × 10⁻⁸ moves transversely at v = 0.6 c across the line of sight. Compute the expected temperature discontinuity ΔT/T observed in the CMB.
The Kaiser‑Stebbins formula gives ΔT/T = 8π Gμ v γ / c², where γ = 1/√(1−v²/c²). For v = 0.6 c, γ = 1/√(1−0.36) = 1/√0.64 = 1.25. Substituting Gμ/c² = 5 × 10⁻⁸ yields ΔT/T = 8π × 5 × 10⁻⁸ × 0.6 × 1.25 ≈ 8π × 3.75 × 10⁻⁸ ≈ 9.42 × 10⁻⁷. Thus the string would produce a temperature step of order 10⁻⁶, well above the instrumental noise of current CMB experiments on degree scales.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for an order‑of‑magnitude estimate of a topological‑defect signature, first identify the relevant dimensionless combination (e.g., Gμ/c² for strings, η for monopoles). Insert the given numerical values, keep π and factors of 8π explicit, and track powers of ten. The final answer should be expressed as a single power of ten multiplied by a factor of order unity; this matches the marking scheme for “estimate” questions.

CMB Spectral Distortions: μ and y Parameters

Spectral distortions of the CMB blackbody arise when energy is injected into the photon‑baryon plasma after the epoch of double‑Compton and bremsstrahlung freeze‑out (z ≲ 2 × 10⁶). Two characteristic shapes appear: the μ‑distortion, produced by energy release at redshifts 5 × 10⁴ ≲ z ≲ 2 × 10⁶ when Compton scattering maintains a Bose‑Einstein spectrum with a non‑zero chemical potential μ, and the y‑distortion, generated at lower redshifts (z ≲ 5 × 10⁴) where Compton scattering is inefficient and the distortion takes the Compton‑y form. The COBE/FIRAS instrument set limits |μ| < 9 × 10⁻⁵ and |y| < 1.5 × 10⁻⁵ (95 % CL). Standard ΛCDM predicts μ ≈ 2 × 10⁻⁸ from the dissipation of acoustic waves (Silk damping) and y ≈ 10⁻⁶ from the reionization epoch and the warm‑hot intergalactic medium. Early energy injection — e.g., decaying dark matter, primordial black‑hole evaporation, or dissipation of small‑scale magnetic fields — enhances μ proportionally to the injected energy fraction Δρ/ρ. A measurement of μ at the 10⁻⁸ level would directly probe the primordial power spectrum on comoving scales k ≈ 10–10⁴ Mpc⁻¹, far smaller than those accessible to CMB anisotropies. The proposed PIXIE and Super‑PIXIE missions aim for σ(μ) ~ 10⁻⁸ and σ(y) ~ 10⁻⁹, turning spectral distortions into a precision cosmological probe. The y‑parameter also encodes the integrated thermal history of baryons; cross‑correlating y‑maps with galaxy surveys isolates the missing‑baryon problem and tests feedback models in galaxy formation.

Redshift‑Space Distortions and the Growth Rate fσ₈

Redshift‑space distortions (RSD) arise because galaxy peculiar velocities add a Doppler shift to the cosmological redshift, anisotropically stretching or squashing the clustering pattern along the line of sight. In linear theory, the galaxy power spectrum in redshift space is Pₛ(k,μ) = (1 + β μ²)² Pᵣ(k), where μ = k∥/k, β = f/b, f = d ln D/d ln a is the linear growth rate, and b is the linear galaxy bias. The combination fσ₈ ≡ f σ₈(z) is directly measurable because the amplitude of the redshift‑space power spectrum scales as (bσ₈)²(1+βμ²)². By fitting the multipole moments (monopole, quadrupole, hexadecapole) of the correlation function or power spectrum, surveys such as BOSS, eBOSS, and DESI constrain fσ₈ at multiple redshifts with ~3–5 % precision. Non‑linear effects — Finger‑of‑God damping from virialized velocities and mode‑coupling — are modeled using perturbation theory (e.g., TNS, EFTofLSS) or emulators calibrated on N‑body simulations. The Alcock‑Paczynski effect, which distorts the apparent shape of the BAO feature if an incorrect cosmology is assumed, is simultaneously fitted via the dilation parameters α∥ and α⊥. Consistency between the expansion history (from BAO) and the growth history (from fσ₈) provides a powerful test of General Relativity on ~100 Mpc scales. A persistent deviation of fσ₈ from the ΛCDM prediction, especially a lower growth rate at z ≈ 0.5–1, would signal either a modification of gravity (μ ≠ 1) or a time‑varying dark‑energy equation of state that alters the growth factor.

Cosmic Voids as Probes of Dark Energy and Modified Gravity

Cosmic voids are large, underdense regions that occupy most of the volume of the universe. Their abundance, size distribution, and internal density profiles are sensitive to the growth of structure and the background expansion. In ΛCDM, the void size function follows a universal scaling when expressed in terms of the linear radius Rᵥ and the variance σ²(Rᵥ) of the density field; the excursion‑set formalism predicts a void abundance n(Rᵥ) ∝ exp[−δᵥ²/(2σ²)] with δᵥ ≈ −2.7. Modified gravity models that enhance the effective Newton constant in low‑density environments (e.g., chameleon screening) produce larger, deeper voids and a steeper void size function. The void‑galaxy cross‑correlation function exhibits a characteristic anisotropic pattern in redshift space: the outflow of galaxies from void centres generates a quadrupole moment that directly measures the product fσ₈ and the void bias bᵥ. Stacking voids also yields the integrated Sachs‑Wolfe (ISW) imprint on the CMB, providing a complementary probe of the time‑varying gravitational potential. Current surveys (SDSS, DES, eBOSS) have measured the void size function and the void‑galaxy RSD, constraining the growth index γ to within ~0.05 and placing limits on the Hu‑Sawicki f(R) model parameter |fR₀| < 10⁻⁵. Future wide‑field surveys (Euclid, LSST, Roman) will increase the void sample by an order of magnitude, enabling a tomographic analysis of void evolution and a percent‑level test of the Poisson equation on ~50 Mpc scales. Combining void statistics with BAO, weak lensing, and cluster counts breaks degeneracies between dark‑energy dynamics and modified‑gravity parameters, making voids a cornerstone of Stage‑IV cosmology.

  • Confusing the μ‑distortion (chemical potential) with the y‑distortion (Compton‑y) and misassigning the redshift ranges where each dominates.
  • Treating the Kaiser formula (1+βμ²)² as exact on all scales; non‑linear Finger‑of‑God damping and mode‑coupling must be modeled for k ≳ 0.1 h Mpc⁻¹.
  • Assuming voids are completely empty; the density contrast at the void centre is typically δ ≈ −0.8, not −1, and the profile matters for RSD and lensing.
  • Neglecting the screening scale in chameleon/f(R) models; void interiors may be unscreened while walls remain screened, altering the effective force law.
  • Using the COBE/FIRAS limits on μ and y as if they were detections; they are upper bounds, and any claimed signal must be compared to the predicted ΛCDM floor.
  • Overlooking that PBH constraints from accretion assume spherical Bondi accretion; realistic angular momentum and radiation feedback can weaken the bounds by orders of magnitude.
  • Equating the string tension bound Gμ/c² < 1.3 × 10⁻⁷ with a detection; it is an exclusion limit that depends on the assumed string network model.

These advanced topics illustrate how precision observations of the CMB spectrum, galaxy clustering in redshift space, and the large‑scale void network jointly test the ΛCDM paradigm and its extensions.

CMB Lensing and the Reconstruction of the Matter Power Spectrum

Gravitational lensing of the cosmic microwave background by the intervening large‑scale structure remaps the primordial temperature and polarization fields. The lensing potential φ(𝐧̂) is a line‑of‑sight integral of the Weyl potential, weighted by the geometric kernel that peaks at redshift z ≈ 2. In harmonic space the lensed temperature multipoles are given by a convolution of the unlensed field with the gradient of φ, generating non‑Gaussian correlations that can be exploited to reconstruct φ via quadratic estimators. The minimum‑variance estimator combines temperature‑temperature (TT), temperature‑E (TE), and E‑E (EE) pairs, yielding a reconstruction noise NLφφ that falls as L−2 on large angular scales. Planck 2018 measured the lensing power spectrum CLφφ with a signal‑to‑noise ratio exceeding 40, constraining the amplitude parameter AL = 1.01 ± 0.06, consistent with the ΛCDM prediction. The reconstructed lensing map correlates with external tracers — galaxy surveys, cosmic infrared background, and 21‑cm intensity maps — providing a direct probe of the matter power spectrum P(k,z) on comoving scales 0.01 h Mpc−1 ≲ k ≲ 1 h Mpc−1. Delensing, the process of subtracting the lensing‑induced B‑mode contamination from the observed polarization, sharpens the search for primordial gravitational waves; current delensing efficiencies of 30–50 % with Planck and SPT‑3G data improve the tensor‑to‑scalar ratio bound r < 0.036 (95 % CL). Future Stage‑4 CMB experiments aim for >80 % delensing, turning lensing from a nuisance into a precision cosmological tool.

Integrated Sachs‑Wolfe Effect and Cross‑Correlation with Large‑Scale Structure

The late‑time Integrated Sachs‑Wolfe (ISW) effect arises when CMB photons traverse evolving gravitational potentials in a universe dominated by dark energy. In ΛCDM the potential decays at z ≲ 1, imparting a net energy shift ΔT/T = 2∫(∂Φ/∂η)dη that correlates with the foreground matter distribution. The angular cross‑power spectrum between CMB temperature maps and a galaxy overdensity field δg(𝐧̂) is CℓTg = (2/π)∫k2dk P(k) IℓISW(k) Iℓg(k), where the ISW kernel IℓISW involves the time derivative of the linear growth factor. Detections using NVSS, 2MASS, SDSS, and WISE catalogs yield a combined significance of 3–4 σ, with the measured amplitude consistent with the ΛCDM prediction AISW = 1.0 ± 0.3. The ISW signal peaks at multipoles ℓ ≲ 30, making it a large‑angular‑scale probe of the dark‑energy equation of state w and its possible time variation. Cross‑correlation with photometric redshift bins enables tomography of the potential decay, tightening constraints on w0 and wa when combined with BAO and supernova data. Systematic contaminants — stellar density gradients, dust extinction, and magnification bias — must be modeled and marginalized; the use of multiple tracers with different bias factors provides a powerful null test for residual systematics.

Worked Example: ISW Amplitude from Cross‑Correlation
A survey measures the angular cross‑power spectrum CℓTg between Planck temperature maps and a galaxy sample with linear bias b = 1.5. At ℓ = 20 the observed value is C20Tg,obs = 1.2 × 10−7 μK. The theoretical ΛCDM prediction for the same bias is C20Tg,th = 1.0 × 10−7 μK. Compute the ISW amplitude parameter AISW and its 1‑σ uncertainty assuming a Gaussian error σ(C20Tg) = 0.3 × 10−7 μK.
The amplitude estimator is AISW = CℓTg,obs / CℓTg,th. Substituting the numbers gives AISW = (1.2 × 10−7) / (1.0 × 10−7) = 1.2. The fractional error propagates as σ(A)/A = σ(C)/C = 0.3/1.2 = 0.25, so σ(A) = 1.2 × 0.25 = 0.30. Hence AISW = 1.20 ± 0.30, consistent with the ΛCDM value of unity at the 0.7 σ level.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the ISW amplitude, remember that the estimator is simply the ratio of observed to theoretical cross‑spectra at a given multipole. The error on the ratio is the fractional error of the observed spectrum (theoretical error is usually neglected in exam problems). Write the formula A = Cobs/Cth and propagate errors as σ(A) = A · σ(Cobs)/Cobs. This avoids unnecessary integrals and earns full marks for the numerical part.

Kinematic Sunyaev‑Zel'dovich Effect as a Probe of Reionization

The kinematic Sunyaev‑Zel'dovich (kSZ) effect is the Doppler shift of CMB photons scattering off free electrons with bulk velocity **v** relative to the CMB rest frame. The temperature fluctuation is ΔT/T = −σT∫ne(**v**·**n̂**)dl, where σT is the Thomson cross‑section and ne the electron density. Unlike the thermal SZ effect, the kSZ signal is frequency‑independent (to first order) and preserves the blackbody spectrum, making it a clean tracer of the momentum field **q** = (1+δe)**v**. During the epoch of reionization (z ≈ 6–10) patchy ionization generates a characteristic kSZ power spectrum peaking at ℓ ≈ 3000 with amplitude DℓkSZ ≈ 1–2 μK2 for a reionization duration Δz ≈ 1–2. Current upper limits from ACT and SPT (D3000 < 2.8 μK2 at 95 % CL) constrain the product of the ionization fraction variance and the velocity power spectrum, disfavoring extremely extended reionization histories (Δz > 5). The pairwise kSZ estimator, which correlates the momentum of galaxy pairs, has yielded a 3–4 σ detection of the kSZ signal at low redshift (z ≈ 0.5) using BOSS and ACT data, providing a direct measurement of the growth rate fσ8 independent of galaxy bias. Future CMB‑S4 and Simons Observatory measurements will push the kSZ signal‑to‑noise above 10, enabling tomographic reconstruction of the ionization history and a percent‑level test of the velocity field predicted by ΛCDM.

Primordial Magnetic Fields and Their Imprints on the CMB

Primordial magnetic fields (PMFs) generated during inflation, phase transitions, or pre‑recombination plasma processes leave distinct signatures on CMB anisotropies. A stochastic PMF with comoving amplitude Bλ smoothed on scale λ = 1 Mpc contributes to the energy‑momentum tensor as an anisotropic stress, sourcing vector and tensor metric perturbations that survive until recombination. The resulting CMB temperature and polarization power spectra receive additional contributions: a passive scalar mode from the magnetic energy density (scaling as knB+3 for a spectral index nB), a compensated vector mode that peaks at ℓ ≈ 1000–2000, and a tensor mode that generates B‑mode polarization on large angular scales. Planck 2015 constraints on the passive mode give B1 Mpc < 4.4 nG (95 % CL) for a nearly scale‑invariant spectrum (nB ≈ −2.9). The vector mode is bounded by the small‑scale temperature power spectrum, yielding B1 Mpc < 1.5 nG for nB = −2.9. Faraday rotation of CMB polarization by a PMF induces a frequency‑dependent rotation angle α(ν) ∝ ν−2∫B∥nedl; current limits on cosmic birefringence (|α| < 0.3°) translate into B1 Mpc < 0.5 nG for a maximally helical field. Joint analysis of temperature, E‑mode, B‑mode, and Faraday rotation data breaks degeneracies between PMF parameters and standard cosmological parameters, making the CMB a unique probe of magnetogenesis in the early universe.

Cosmic Birefringence and Parity‑Violating Physics in CMB Polarization

Cosmic birefringence is a rotation of the linear polarization plane of CMB photons as they propagate through a parity‑violating medium, such as a pseudoscalar field φ coupled to the electromagnetic Chern‑Simons term (φ/4M)FμνF̃μν. The rotation angle α = Δφ/(2M) is frequency‑independent, distinguishing it from Faraday rotation. A uniform α converts E‑mode polarization into B‑mode, generating TB and EB cross‑spectra that vanish in standard ΛCDM. The observed spectra become CℓTB,obs = CℓTEsin(2α) and CℓEB,obs = (CℓEE−CℓBB)sin(2α)cos(2α)/2. Planck 2018 polarization data constrain the isotropic rotation to α = 0.30° ± 0.11° (68 % CL), a 2.7 σ hint of parity violation; however, systematic uncertainties in the absolute polarization angle calibration of the detectors dominate the error budget. Anisotropic birefringence, where α(𝐧̂) fluctuates across the sky, produces a non‑zero four‑point function (trispectrum) that can be reconstructed with a quadratic estimator analogous to lensing. Current limits on the anisotropic amplitude are Aα < 0.02 rad2 at ℓ ≈ 100. A definitive detection of isotropic or anisotropic birefringence would point to axion‑like fields, Chern‑Simons gravity, or other extensions of the Standard Model that violate parity, providing a rare window into fundamental physics at energy scales far beyond terrestrial accelerators.

  • Confusing the frequency dependence of Faraday rotation (∝ ν−2) with the achromatic nature of cosmic birefringence.
  • Assuming the kSZ effect has a thermal spectral distortion; it is a pure Doppler shift and leaves the blackbody spectrum intact.
  • Neglecting the vector mode contribution of primordial magnetic fields when only the passive scalar mode is considered in CMB power‑spectrum fits.
  • Treating the ISW cross‑correlation amplitude as a direct measurement of dark‑energy density without marginalizing over galaxy bias and magnification bias.
  • Using the lensing reconstruction noise formula for temperature‑only estimators when polarization data (EE, TE) are available, which significantly lowers NLφφ.
  • Overlooking that delensing efficiency depends on the lensing‑potential reconstruction noise and the experimental beam; a high‑resolution, low‑noise polarization map is essential for >80 % delensing.
  • Equating the pairwise kSZ estimator signal with the thermal SZ signal; the former measures the momentum field, the latter the pressure field, and they have different redshift kernels.
The advanced sections above complete the cosmology module with mechanisms, observational signatures, and quantitative tools that appear in competitive examinations.

Thermal Sunyaev‑Zel’dovich Effect and Cluster Cosmology

The thermal Sunyaev‑Zel’dovich (tSZ) effect arises when cosmic microwave background photons inverse‑Compton scatter off hot electrons in the intracluster medium of galaxy clusters. The scattering imprints a characteristic spectral distortion: a decrement at frequencies below 217 GHz and an increment above, with a null at the crossover frequency where the blackbody spectrum is unchanged. The amplitude of the distortion is quantified by the Compton‑y parameter, y = ∫ (kBTe / mec2) ne σT dl, where Te is the electron temperature, ne the electron number density, σT the Thomson cross‑section, and the integral runs along the line of sight. For massive clusters (M500 ≳ 5 × 1014 M⊙) typical central y values reach 10−4–10−3.

Planck’s all‑sky tSZ survey (2015, 2018) produced a catalogue of over 1 600 clusters detected via their tSZ signal, providing a mass‑selected sample that is largely independent of optical richness. The tSZ power spectrum, measured by Planck, ACT, and SPT, constrains the amplitude of matter fluctuations σ8 and the matter density Ωm through the scaling relation Y500–M500. Current analyses combine tSZ cluster counts with the tSZ angular power spectrum to break degeneracies between σ8 and the hydrostatic mass bias (1 − b). A typical result from Planck 2018 is σ8(Ωm/0.31)0.3 = 0.78 ± 0.01, which is in tension with primary CMB inferences at the 2–3 σ level.

Key systematic uncertainties include: (i) the hydrostatic mass bias, calibrated with X‑ray and weak‑lensing masses; (ii) the pressure profile shape, often modelled with a generalized Navarro‑Frenk‑White (GNFW) form; (iii) contamination from the kinematic SZ (kSZ) effect and from radio/infrared point sources; and (iv) the impact of cluster selection functions on the inferred cosmology. Future surveys (Simons Observatory, CMB‑S4, eROSITA) will improve the tSZ signal‑to‑noise by an order of magnitude, enabling tomographic tSZ measurements that map the growth of structure as a function of redshift and providing percent‑level tests of the ΛCDM growth history.

Ordinary and Early Integrated Sachs‑Wolfe Effects

The Sachs‑Wolfe (SW) effect describes the gravitational redshift/blueshift of CMB photons as they climb out of (or fall into) potential wells at the surface of last scattering. On angular scales larger than the sound horizon at recombination (ℓ ≲ 30) the temperature anisotropy is dominated by the ordinary SW term: ΔT/T = (1/3)Φ, where Φ is the Newtonian potential. This produces the characteristic plateau in the CMB angular power spectrum and directly probes the primordial curvature perturbation amplitude.

The integrated Sachs‑Wolfe (ISW) effect accumulates along the photon path when the gravitational potential evolves with time. Two distinct regimes exist. The early ISW (eISW) occurs around recombination when radiation still contributes appreciably to the energy density; the decay of potentials during the radiation‑to‑matter transition boosts the first acoustic peak by roughly 10 % and shifts its position. The late ISW (lISW) arises after dark energy dominates (z ≲ 1), when potentials decay again. The lISW generates large‑scale temperature anisotropies that are correlated with the large‑scale structure of the universe. Cross‑correlating CMB maps with galaxy surveys (e.g., NVSS, SDSS, DES, WISE) yields a detection of the lISW at 3–4 σ, providing an independent probe of dark energy and the growth rate.

Quantitatively, the ISW contribution to the temperature angular power spectrum can be written as CℓISW = (2/π) ∫ k2 dk PΦ(k) [∫ dη (dΦ/dη) jℓ(k(η0−η))]2, where η is conformal time and jℓ are spherical Bessel functions. The eISW term peaks at ℓ ≈ 200, while the lISW term is confined to ℓ ≲ 30. The cross‑power spectrum between CMB temperature and a galaxy overdensity field δg is CℓTg = (2/π) ∫ k2 dk PΦδ(k) [∫ dη (dΦ/dη) jℓ(k(η0−η))] [∫ dη b(z) D(z) jℓ(k(η0−η))], where b(z) is the galaxy bias and D(z) the linear growth factor. Measurements of CℓTg constrain the dark‑energy equation‑of‑state parameter w and the growth index γ.

Worked Example: ISW Cross‑Correlation Amplitude
A galaxy survey with linear bias b = 1.5 covers redshift range 0.2 < z < 0.8. Assuming a flat ΛCDM model with Ωm = 0.31, σ8 = 0.81, and w = −1, estimate the expected signal‑to‑noise (S/N) of the ISW–galaxy cross‑correlation for multipoles ℓ = 2–30. Use the approximation that the cumulative (S/N)2 ≈ Σℓ (2ℓ+1) fsky [CℓTg]2 / [CℓTT Cℓgg], with fsky = 0.5.
1. Compute the theoretical CℓTg using a Boltzmann code (e.g., CLASS or CAMB) for the given cosmology and redshift window. 2. Obtain CℓTT (including primary + ISW + noise) and Cℓgg = b2 Cℓδδ + 1/ n̄ (shot noise). 3. For each ℓ = 2…30 evaluate the term (2ℓ+1) fsky [CℓTg]2 / [CℓTT Cℓgg]. 4. Sum over ℓ and take the square root. For the stated survey, typical forecasts give (S/N) ≈ 3.5–4.0, consistent with published DES‑Planck cross‑correlation results.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the physical origin of a large‑scale CMB feature, first identify the angular scale: ℓ ≲ 30 points to ordinary SW or late ISW; ℓ ≈ 200 points to early ISW or the first acoustic peak. Remember that the ordinary SW term is exactly (1/3)Φ only for adiabatic perturbations in a matter‑dominated universe; any deviation (e.g., isocurvature, curvature) modifies the coefficient. For cross‑correlation problems, write down the Limber‑approximated integral and note the dependence on bias, growth factor, and the time derivative of the potential.

Cosmic Microwave Background Foreground Component Separation

Accurate cosmological inference from CMB data requires the removal of astrophysical foregrounds that dominate the microwave sky at frequencies above 100 GHz (thermal dust) and below 70 GHz (synchrotron, free‑free, and anomalous microwave emission). The Planck mission employed four independent component‑separation pipelines — Commander, SMICA, NILC, and SEVEM — each exploiting different statistical assumptions. Commander uses a Bayesian parametric model fitting the spectral energy distributions (SEDs) of each component pixel by pixel, with priors on spectral indices. SMICA (Spectral Matching Independent Component Analysis) operates in harmonic space, assuming statistical independence of components and maximizing a likelihood based on the covariance of multi‑frequency maps. NILC (Needlet Internal Linear Combination) constructs a minimum‑variance linear combination of frequency maps in needlet (wavelet) space, preserving spatial localization. SEVEM (Template Fitting) fits external templates (e.g., Haslam 408 MHz map for synchrotron, Planck 857 GHz map for dust) to the data.

All four pipelines produce consistent CMB temperature and polarization maps at the level of a few μK‑arcmin in the cleanest regions, but they differ in residual foreground power on small scales and near the Galactic plane. The Planck 2018 likelihood uses a consensus mask (≈ 78 % sky fraction) and propagates the spread among pipelines into systematic uncertainties on cosmological parameters. For polarization, the dominant foregrounds are synchrotron (spectral index βs ≈ −3.1) and thermal dust (βd ≈ 1.55). The component‑separation challenge intensifies for the search for primordial B‑modes, where the tensor‑to‑scalar ratio r ≲ 0.01 corresponds to a B‑mode amplitude of ≈ 0.1 μK‑arcmin, far below the polarized dust foreground even at high latitudes.

Future experiments (Simons Observatory, CMB‑S4, LiteBIRD) will observe in 10–20 frequency bands spanning 20–400 GHz, enabling blind component separation with parametric fitting of spatially varying SEDs. Machine‑learning approaches (e.g., deep neural networks trained on simulations) are being explored to capture non‑linear mixing and to marginalize over foreground model uncertainties. The ultimate goal is to reduce foreground residuals in the B‑mode power spectrum to Δr < 0.001, requiring control of polarized dust spectral‑index variations at the Δβd ≈ 0.01 level.

Isocurvature Perturbations and Their CMB Signatures

In addition to the adiabatic curvature perturbations that dominate the standard ΛCDM model, inflationary scenarios with multiple light fields can generate isocurvature (entropy) perturbations. These are fluctuations in the relative number densities of different species (cold dark matter, baryons, neutrinos, photons) that leave the total curvature perturbation unchanged on super‑horizon scales. The primordial power spectrum is then described by a matrix of auto‑ and cross‑spectra: Pℛℛ, PSS, PℛS, where ℛ is the curvature perturbation and S denotes a specific isocurvature mode (CDM, baryon, neutrino density, or neutrino velocity).

CMB temperature and polarization anisotropies respond differently to each mode. CDM isocurvature (Sc) produces a characteristic peak in the temperature power spectrum at ℓ ≈ 200, out of phase with the adiabatic peaks, and a large‑scale E‑mode polarization signal. Baryon isocurvature (Sb) is tightly constrained by big‑bang nucleosynthesis and CMB acoustic peak ratios. Neutrino density isocurvature (Sν) and neutrino velocity isocurvature (Sνv) affect the phase of acoustic oscillations and the damping tail. Planck 2018 temperature and polarization data constrain the fractional contribution of uncorrelated CDM isocurvature to the primordial power to be αc = PSS/(Pℛℛ+PSS) < 0.038 (95 % CL). For fully correlated (or anti‑correlated) isocurvature the bounds tighten to |αc| < 0.001. These limits strongly disfavour simple axion models with a large decay constant unless the axion field is tuned to produce negligible isocurvature.

The presence of isocurvature also modifies the interpretation of the CMB lensing potential and the matter power spectrum, because the initial conditions for structure growth differ. Joint analyses of CMB, large‑scale structure, and 21‑cm intensity mapping can break degeneracies between isocurvature amplitude and the neutrino mass sum Σmν. Future CMB‑S4 polarization measurements will improve the sensitivity to the isocurvature‑induced E‑mode signal by an order of magnitude, providing a decisive test of multi‑field inflationary dynamics.

Relativistic Corrections to CMB Anisotropies: Doppler Boosting and Aberration

Our motion relative to the CMB rest frame (v ≈ 369 km s−1 toward (l,b) = (264°,48°)) introduces two distinct relativistic effects on the observed anisotropy field. Doppler boosting (also called aberration) modulates the apparent temperature by a factor (1 + β·n̂)3 where β = v/c ≈ 1.23 × 10−3. To first order in β, this generates a dipolar modulation of the temperature power spectrum: the observed aℓm coefficients are coupled to neighboring multipoles, producing off‑diagonal correlations ⟨aℓm aℓ' m'⟩ ∝ β. The effect is a large‑scale dipole asymmetry in the power spectrum of order 10−3, which has been measured by Planck and is consistent with the known Solar motion.

Aberration changes the apparent direction of incoming photons, effectively remapping the sky by a small angle δθ ≈ β sinθ. In harmonic space this leads to a mixing of multipoles: aℓmobs = aℓm + β Σℓ' m' Kℓℓ' m m' aℓ' m', where the kernel K couples ℓ to ℓ±1. The net result is a shift of power from large to small scales (and vice versa) that mimics a change in the scalar spectral index ns of order Δns ≈ 0.001. If uncorrected, this bias would affect high‑precision determinations of ns from future CMB‑S4 data.

Higher‑order relativistic corrections include the time‑delay (Shapiro) effect, gravitational lensing by the large‑scale potential (already treated in the lensing section), and the Rees‑Sciama effect (non‑linear ISW). For most current analyses the Doppler boosting and aberration terms dominate the systematic error budget for ℓ ≳ 2000. The standard mitigation is to de‑boost the observed maps to the CMB rest frame using the measured dipole, or to include the β‑dependent coupling matrix in the likelihood. The Planck 2018 likelihood incorporates a first‑order aberration correction; CMB‑S4 forecasts indicate that second‑order terms (β2) will become relevant for r < 0.001 searches.

  • Confusing the tSZ spectral null (217 GHz) with the kSZ frequency independence; the kSZ is a pure Doppler shift and has no spectral distortion.
  • Assuming the ordinary Sachs‑Wolfe plateau is solely due to the (1/3)Φ term; early ISW contributes significantly to the first peak height.
  • Treating all foreground separation methods as equivalent; Commander’s parametric priors can bias the CMB map if the SED model is wrong, while NILC’s needlet ILC is more robust but leaves higher residuals near the Galactic plane.
  • Neglecting the correlation between isocurvature and adiabatic modes; a non‑zero cross‑spectrum PℛS changes the sign of the isocurvature contribution to the temperature power spectrum.
  • Ignoring aberration when fitting for ns at ℓ > 2000; the induced bias mimics a tilt of Δns ≈ 0.001, comparable to the target precision of next‑generation experiments.
  • Equating the late ISW signal with the early ISW; they have opposite signs on large scales and different redshift kernels.
  • Using the tSZ y‑parameter as a direct mass proxy without marginalizing over the hydrostatic mass bias and pressure‑profile uncertainties.

The advanced sections above complete the cosmology module with mechanisms, observational signatures, and quantitative tools that appear in competitive examinations.

CMB Lensing Delensing and the Search for Primordial B‑Modes

Gravitational lensing of the CMB by large‑scale structure converts primordial E‑mode polarization into a secondary B‑mode signal that peaks at multipoles ℓ ≈ 1000 with an amplitude of roughly 5 µK². This lensing B‑mode is a guaranteed foreground for any measurement of the primordial tensor‑to‑scalar ratio r, because the primordial B‑mode from inflation peaks at ℓ ≈ 80 and falls rapidly toward higher ℓ. Delensing is the process of estimating the lensing potential φ from the observed CMB maps (or from external tracers such as the cosmic infrared background) and subtracting the lensing‑induced B‑mode from the data. The residual lensing power after delensing is often parameterised by the delensing efficiency ε = 1 − Cℓ^BB,res / Cℓ^BB,lens. Current experiments such as the Simons Observatory achieve ε ≈ 0.5–0.6 using internal quadratic estimators, while forecasts for CMB‑S4 with external large‑scale‑structure tracers predict ε ≳ 0.9. The quadratic estimator reconstructs φ from the off‑diagonal correlations ⟨aℓm aℓ′m′⟩ induced by lensing; its noise power Nℓ^φφ determines the delensing efficiency. Iterative maximum‑likelihood delensing can further reduce bias from the estimator’s own noise, but at the cost of increased computational complexity. A key systematic is the mean‑field bias arising from anisotropic noise and masking; it is removed by Monte‑Carlo simulations that match the survey geometry. Residual foregrounds (thermal dust, synchrotron) also leak into the φ reconstruction and must be marginalised. The ultimate goal is to push the statistical error on r below σ(r) ≈ 0.001, which requires both high delensing efficiency and exquisite control of polarized foregrounds.

Higher‑Order CMB Statistics: Lensing‑Induced Trispectrum and Non‑Gaussianity

While the CMB power spectrum captures two‑point statistics, the connected four‑point function (trispectrum) encodes information about the non‑Gaussianity generated by gravitational lensing. Lensing remaps the primordial Gaussian field, producing a non‑zero trispectrum of the form Tℓ1ℓ2ℓ3ℓ4 ∝ Cℓ1^φφ Cℓ2^TT + permutations. This signal is distinct from primordial non‑Gaussianity (local, equilateral, orthogonal shapes) because its angular dependence is fixed by the geometry of the lensing kernel. The lensing trispectrum has been measured by Planck through the reconstruction noise bias Nℓ^(0) of the quadratic estimator; the measured Nℓ^(0) agrees with the theoretical prediction to within a few percent, providing a consistency test of the ΛCDM lensing potential. Higher‑order biases Nℓ^(1) and Nℓ^(2) arise from the non‑linear response of the estimator to the lensed field and must be subtracted for unbiased φ power spectra. Future experiments will use the trispectrum to constrain the sum of neutrino masses Σmν, because massive neutrinos suppress the small‑scale lensing potential and thereby reduce the trispectrum amplitude. The trispectrum also offers a probe of primordial magnetic fields, which generate a characteristic parity‑odd component. Analysing the trispectrum requires careful treatment of mask‑induced mode coupling; the standard approach is to compute the disconnected Gaussian bias analytically and subtract it using simulations. The signal‑to‑noise of the lensing trispectrum for CMB‑S4 is expected to exceed 100, making it a powerful consistency check and a complementary constraint on cosmological parameters.

Delensing Bias Estimation
A quadratic estimator reconstructs the lensing potential φ with a reconstruction noise power Nℓ^φφ = 1 × 10⁻⁷ at ℓ = 100. The true lensing power spectrum at that multipole is Cℓ^φφ = 2 × 10⁻⁷. Assuming the delensing efficiency is ε = 1 − Nℓ^φφ / Cℓ^φφ, compute the residual lensing B‑mode power fraction relative to the original lensing B‑mode power.
The delensing efficiency ε = 1 − (1 × 10⁻⁷) / (2 × 10⁻⁷) = 1 − 0.5 = 0.5. The residual lensing B‑mode power fraction is 1 − ε = 0.5, i.e. 50 % of the original lensing B‑mode power remains after delensing.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a delensing efficiency or residual power, first identify the reconstruction noise Nℓ^φφ and the true lensing power Cℓ^φφ. The efficiency formula ε = 1 − Nℓ^φφ / Cℓ^φφ is standard; plug numbers directly. Remember that the residual fraction is simply Nℓ^φφ / Cℓ^φφ. For trispectrum questions, recall that the lensing trispectrum is fully determined by the lensing potential power spectrum and the unlensed CMB spectra — no free shape parameters.

Cosmic Infrared Background Anisotropies and Their Contamination of CMB Measurements

The cosmic infrared background (CIB) originates from dust‑obscured star‑forming galaxies at redshifts 1 ≲ z ≲ 3. Its angular power spectrum follows a power law Cℓ^CIB ∝ ℓ⁻¹·² on degree scales and peaks at ℓ ≈ 3000 with an amplitude of a few µK² at 150 GHz. Because the CIB traces the same large‑scale structure that lenses the CMB, it is strongly correlated with the CMB lensing potential (cross‑correlation coefficient r ≈ 0.8). This correlation makes the CIB both a valuable external tracer for delensing and a contaminant for primordial B‑mode searches: the CIB’s polarized emission is negligible, but its intensity fluctuations leak into component‑separated CMB maps through imperfect frequency scaling, especially when the CIB spectral energy distribution (SED) deviates from a single modified blackbody. The CIB SED is typically modelled as a greybody with temperature T ≈ 20 K and emissivity index β ≈ 1.5, but variations across the sky introduce a frequency‑dependent bias in the cleaned CMB map. Multi‑frequency component separation methods such as Commander and NILC marginalise over CIB SED parameters; however, residual CIB power can mimic a lensing‑like B‑mode signal at ℓ > 500. Cross‑correlating the cleaned CMB map with external CIB maps (e.g., from Herschel or Planck 857 GHz) provides a diagnostic: a significant cross‑spectrum indicates residual CIB contamination. Future CMB‑S4 analyses plan to use the CIB as a high‑signal‑to‑noise tracer for delensing while simultaneously fitting its SED parameters jointly with the CMB to minimise bias on r.

Cross‑Correlation of CMB Lensing with Galaxy Surveys for Growth Rate Constraints

The CMB lensing potential φ is a line‑of‑sight integral of the matter overdensity weighted by the lensing kernel, which peaks at z ≈ 2.
