Galaxy surveys such as DESI, Euclid, and LSST measure the projected galaxy overdensity δg with a known redshift distribution n(z). The cross‑power spectrum Cℓ^φg = ∫ dz (Wφ(z) Wg(z) / χ²) Pδδ(k = ℓ/χ, z) directly probes the growth of structure, where Wφ and Wg are the lensing and galaxy kernels, χ is the comoving distance, and Pδδ is the matter power spectrum. Because the CMB lensing kernel is broad and well‑calibrated, the cross‑correlation breaks the degeneracy between galaxy bias b(z) and the amplitude of matter fluctuations σ₈. The observable quantity is often expressed as the ratio Cℓ^φg / √(Cℓ^φφ Cℓ^gg), which is independent of linear bias on large scales. Redshift‑space distortions in the galaxy sample introduce an additional μ² dependence (μ = cos θ) that can be modelled to extract the growth rate fσ₈. Systematic effects include photometric redshift outliers, magnification bias, and intrinsic alignments of galaxies; these are mitigated by using spectroscopic subsamples for calibration and by marginalising over nuisance parameters in the likelihood. Forecasts indicate that the combination of CMB‑S4 lensing with DESI‑like galaxy surveys will measure fσ₈ to ≈ 2 % precision across 0.5 < z < 1.5, providing a stringent test of general relativity on cosmological scales.

The Impact of Reionization Patchiness on the Kinematic Sunyaev‑Zel'dovich Power Spectrum

During the epoch of reionization (z ≈ 6–10), ionised bubbles expand into the neutral intergalactic medium. The patchy distribution of free electrons generates a kinematic Sunyaev‑Zel'dovich (kSZ) signal because the bulk velocity of the ionised gas Doppler‑shifts CMB photons. The resulting kSZ power spectrum peaks at ℓ ≈ 3000 with an amplitude that depends on the duration Δz of reionization and the typical bubble size R_b. For a “late, rapid” reionization (Δz ≈ 1, R_b ≈ 10 Mpc), the patchy kSZ power at ℓ = 3000 is Dℓ^patchy ≈ 2 µK²; for an “early, extended” scenario (Δz ≈ 3, R_b ≈ 5 Mpc) it can reach ≈ 5 µK². The homogeneous (post‑reionization) kSZ contribution from the late‑time velocity field adds ≈ 1 µK² at the same multipole. Current upper limits from the South Pole Telescope (SPT‑3G) and ACT constrain Dℓ^patchy < 3 µK² at 95 % C.L., favouring a relatively rapid reionization. The kSZ signal is frequency‑independent (pure Doppler shift), so it cannot be separated by multi‑frequency component separation; instead, it is isolated statistically by cross‑correlating CMB maps with 21‑cm intensity maps or galaxy surveys that trace the ionisation field. Future 21‑cm experiments (HERA, SKA) will map the neutral hydrogen distribution, enabling a direct measurement of the patchy kSZ cross‑spectrum and thereby constraining the reionization topology. The patchy kSZ also contributes to the CMB temperature trispectrum, offering an additional higher‑order probe of the ionisation history.

  • Confusing the delensing efficiency formula ε = 1 − Nℓ^φφ / Cℓ^φφ with the residual fraction; the residual is Nℓ^φφ / Cℓ^φφ.
  • Assuming the lensing trispectrum has free shape parameters like primordial non‑Gaussianity; its shape is fully fixed by the lensing kernel.
  • Treating the CIB as a purely unpolarized contaminant; while its polarized fraction is tiny, intensity‑to‑polarisation leakage through beam mismatch can produce spurious B‑modes.
  • Neglecting the cross‑correlation coefficient between CIB and CMB lensing when using CIB as a delensing tracer; a low r reduces delensing gain.
  • Equating the homogeneous kSZ power with the patchy kSZ power; they have different redshift kernels and scale dependencies.
  • Ignoring magnification bias in galaxy‑CMB lensing cross‑correlations; it adds a term proportional to the galaxy number‑count slope.
  • Using a single modified blackbody for the CIB SED across all frequencies; spatial variations in dust temperature and emissivity index cause frequency‑dependent residuals.

CMB Acoustic Peak Structure and Cosmological Parameter Degeneracies

The temperature anisotropy power spectrum of the cosmic microwave background exhibits a series of acoustic peaks whose positions, amplitudes, and spacing encode the physics of the photon‑baryon fluid before recombination. The fundamental scale is the sound horizon at decoupling, rs ≈ 147 Mpc, which sets the angular scale of the first peak through the angular diameter distance to the last‑scattering surface, DA. A larger DA (e.g. lower H0 or positive curvature) shifts peaks to lower multipoles, while a smaller DA moves them to higher multipoles. The peak heights are governed by the baryon‑to‑photon ratio: higher baryon density Ωbh² enhances odd peaks (compression) relative to even peaks (rarefaction) because baryons add inertia to the fluid. Cold dark matter density Ωch² controls the early Integrated Sachs‑Wolfe effect and the overall driving of oscillations, altering the relative heights of the first three peaks. Silk damping, caused by photon diffusion, suppresses power at multipoles ℓ ≳ 1500 and depends on the baryon density and the recombination history. These dependencies create well‑known degeneracies: the geometric degeneracy between H0 and curvature Ωk leaves the peak positions nearly unchanged; the baryon‑loading degeneracy between Ωbh² and the scalar spectral index ns can mimic similar changes in the odd/even peak ratio; and the early‑ISW degeneracy between Ωch² and the dark‑energy equation of state w affects the first peak height. Planck temperature and polarization data break most of these degeneracies by measuring the damping tail, the polarization‑temperature cross‑spectrum, and the lensing‑induced smoothing of the peaks, yielding H0 = 67.4 ± 0.5 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹, Ωbh² = 0.02237 ± 0.00015, and Ωch² = 0.120 ± 0.001 in the base ΛCDM model.

CMB Likelihood Construction and Bayesian Parameter Estimation

Modern cosmological parameter inference from the CMB relies on a Bayesian framework where the posterior P(θ|d) ∝ L(d|θ) π(θ) is explored with Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) samplers. The likelihood L for the Planck temperature and polarization maps is not a simple Gaussian in the power spectra because of mode coupling from the mask and inhomogeneous noise. Two complementary approaches are used: the exact pixel‑based likelihood at low multipoles (ℓ ≤ 30) and a pseudo‑Cℓ Gaussian approximation at higher ℓ, corrected by the Hamimeche‑Lewis (HL) transformation to account for the non‑Gaussian distribution of the bandpowers. The Planck 2018 likelihood (Plik) incorporates nuisance parameters for unresolved foregrounds (thermal dust, cosmic infrared background, point sources, kinetic and thermal Sunyaev‑Zel’dovich), calibration uncertainties (0.25 % for 100 GHz, 0.1 % for 143 GHz), and beam eigenmode errors. These nuisance parameters are either analytically marginalised (for foreground amplitudes with Gaussian priors) or sampled jointly with the cosmological parameters. The sampling is performed with codes such as CosmoMC or MontePython, which implement the Metropolis‑Hastings algorithm with a fast‑slow parameter splitting to accelerate convergence. Convergence is diagnosed using the Gelman‑Rubin R‑1 statistic (requiring R‑1 < 0.01) and by checking the effective sample size for each parameter. Posterior summaries are reported as mean ± 68 % credible intervals; tensions between datasets are quantified with the suspiciousness statistic or the parameter‑difference χ². The Planck baseline ΛCDM fit yields χ²min ≈ 2760 for 2765 degrees of freedom, indicating an excellent fit, while extensions such as Neff or w are constrained by the same machinery with additional priors.

Examiner's trap
A question states: "The geometric degeneracy in the CMB power spectrum arises because changing the Hubble constant and the spatial curvature can leave the angular diameter distance to last scattering unchanged. Explain why this degeneracy is broken by adding CMB lensing information."
CMB lensing measures the integrated matter distribution along the line of sight, which depends on the physical matter density Ωm and the expansion history, not only on the angular diameter distance. The lensing potential power spectrum Cℓφφ is sensitive to the growth of structure and the distance to the lensing kernel peak at z ≈ 2. Changing H0 and Ωk while keeping DA fixed alters the matter‑radiation equality scale and the late‑time growth factor, thereby modifying the lensing amplitude. Including the lensing likelihood in the joint analysis therefore lifts the geometric degeneracy, as demonstrated by the Planck 2018 results where H0 and Ωk constraints tighten significantly when lensing is added.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks about degeneracies, first identify which observables (peak positions, heights, damping tail, lensing) are being combined. Write down the primary parameter pair (e.g. H0–Ωk) and the physical reason the degeneracy exists (fixed angular diameter distance). Then state the additional probe that breaks it (lensing, BAO, supernovae) and the physical quantity that probe measures (growth, absolute distance). This structured answer scores full marks.

21‑cm Intensity Mapping of the Dark Ages and Cosmic Dawn

The hyperfine transition of neutral hydrogen at 21 cm (1420 MHz) provides a unique window into the Universe before reionization. The differential brightness temperature relative to the CMB is δTb ≈ 27 mK × (1 + z)1/2 (Ωbh²/0.023) [(0.15/Ωmh²)(1 + z)/10]1/2 (1 − Tγ/Ts) (1 + δ), where Ts is the spin temperature. During the Dark Ages (z ≈ 30–200) the gas is colder than the CMB (Ts ≈ Tk < Tγ), producing a strong absorption signal of order −200 mK. At Cosmic Dawn (z ≈ 15–30) the first stars emit Lyman‑α photons that couple Ts to the kinetic temperature Tk (Wouthuysen‑Field effect), driving the signal into deep absorption (≈ −150 mK) before X‑ray heating raises Tk above Tγ and the signal turns into emission. The global (sky‑averaged) signal has been targeted by experiments such as EDGES, SARAS, and LEDA; EDGES reported a flattened absorption trough centred at 78 MHz (z ≈ 17) with an amplitude ≈ −500 mK, deeper than standard ΛCDM predictions, sparking debate about possible exotic physics (e.g. dark matter‑baryon scattering). Fluctuation measurements (the 21‑cm power spectrum) are pursued by interferometers such as HERA, LOFAR, MWA, and the future SKA‑Low. Foregrounds — Galactic synchrotron (∝ ν−2.5), extragalactic point sources, and free‑free emission — exceed the cosmological signal by 4–5 orders of magnitude. Component separation exploits the smooth frequency dependence of foregrounds versus the structured cosmological signal; techniques include polynomial fitting, Gaussian Process regression, and the delay‑transform method. Current upper limits on the 21‑cm power spectrum at z ≈ 8–10 are Δ²(k) < 10³ mK² at k ≈ 0.1 h Mpc⁻¹, already constraining the timing and morphology of reionization. A detection of the Dark Ages absorption trough would directly measure the primordial power spectrum on comoving scales k ≈ 10–100 Mpc⁻¹, probing inflationary models inaccessible to the CMB.

Dark Matter Self‑Interactions and Small‑Scale Structure Anomalies

Cold dark matter (CDM) simulations predict cuspy Navarro‑Frenk‑White (NFW) density profiles (ρ ∝ r−1) in the centres of haloes and a large abundance of subhaloes. Observations of dwarf and low‑surface‑brightness galaxies, however, reveal constant‑density cores (ρ ≈ constant) and a factor of ∼10 fewer satellites than predicted — the core‑cusp and missing‑satellites problems. The too‑big‑to‑fail problem adds that the most massive subhaloes in Milky‑Way‑like haloes are too dense to host any observed bright satellites. Self‑interacting dark matter (SIDM) introduces a scattering cross‑section per unit mass σ/m that thermalises the inner halo, creating an isothermal core of size rcore ≈ (σ/m) (ρ0 v0)−1. For dwarf galaxies with velocity dispersions v ≈ 10 km s⁻¹, a cross‑section σ/m ≈ 1 cm² g⁻¹ yields cores of ∼1 kpc, matching observations. The same cross‑section must satisfy cluster constraints: the Bullet Cluster (1E 0657‑56) limits σ/m < 1.25 cm² g⁻¹ at relative velocities ≈ 4000 km s⁻¹, while relaxed clusters prefer σ/m ≲ 0.1 cm² g⁻¹ to avoid excessive core formation. Velocity‑dependent models (e.g. dark photon exchange with a light mediator) naturally produce σ/m ∝ v−4 at high velocities, reconciling dwarf and cluster scales. Recent hydrodynamical simulations with baryonic feedback show that supernova‑driven outflows can also create cores, but they struggle to simultaneously solve all three anomalies across the full mass range. The current consensus from combined dwarf, low‑surface‑brightness, and cluster data is σ/m < 0.1 cm² g⁻¹ at v ≈ 30 km s⁻¹ (95 % C.L.), with a possible window 0.1–1 cm² g⁻¹ at lower velocities if the mediator mass is ∼10 MeV. Future observations of ultra‑faint dwarfs with JWST and 30‑m class telescopes, together with precise strong‑lensing measurements of cluster cores, will tighten these bounds.

Modified Gravity Screening Mechanisms: Vainshtein, Chameleon, Symmetron

Many infrared modifications of General Relativity introduce an extra scalar degree of freedom that mediates a fifth force. To satisfy stringent Solar System tests (e.g. Cassini bound on the post‑Newtonian parameter γ − 1 < 2.3 × 10⁻⁵), this force must be screened in high‑density environments. Three principal screening mechanisms operate in different theories. The Vainshtein mechanism, present in massive gravity, Galileon, and Horndeski models, relies on non‑linear derivative self‑interactions of the scalar. Inside the Vainshtein radius rV = (rs/Λ³)1/3 (with rs the Schwarzschild radius and Λ the strong‑coupling scale), the scalar’s kinetic term is dominated by non‑linearities, suppressing its coupling to matter by a factor (r/rV)3/2. For the Sun, rV ≈ 100 pc, so planetary orbits are unaffected. The Chameleon mechanism, realised in f(R) gravity and scalar‑tensor theories with a density‑dependent effective potential, gives the scalar a mass meff that grows with ambient density. In dense regions (Earth, Sun) the Compton wavelength λC = meff−1 becomes sub‑millimetre, exponentially suppressing the fifth force. In cosmological voids the scalar is light and mediates a long‑range force, enhancing structure growth. The Symmetron mechanism uses a symmetry‑breaking potential V(φ) = −½ μ²φ² + ¼ λφ⁴ with a matter coupling that triggers symmetry restoration at high density. In dense environments the field sits at φ = 0 (symmetry unbroken) and decouples; in low‑density regions it acquires a vacuum expectation value and mediates a fifth force. Observational signatures differ: Vainshtein screening predicts no deviation in cluster dynamics but a modified gravitational wave speed (constrained to |cgw/c − 1| < 10⁻¹⁵ by GW170817); Chameleon models produce a scale‑dependent enhancement of the matter power spectrum and a distinctive cluster density profile (tested with X‑ray and lensing data); Symmetron models predict a sharp transition in the force law at the screening radius, potentially detectable in galaxy velocity dispersions. Current constraints from CMB lensing, redshift‑space distortions, and cluster counts limit the Chameleon parameter |fR0| < 10⁻⁶ (95 % C.L.) for f(R) gravity, while Vainshtein‑type theories are primarily bounded by the gravitational wave speed measurement.

  • Confusing the geometric degeneracy (H₀–Ωₖ) with the baryon‑loading degeneracy (Ω_b h²–nₛ); they affect different peak features.
  • Assuming the Planck likelihood is a simple Gaussian in Cℓ; the low‑ℓ pixel likelihood and the Hamimeche‑Lewis correction are essential for accurate posteriors.
  • Treating the 21‑cm global signal and the fluctuation power spectrum as interchangeable probes; they constrain different combinations of astrophysical parameters.
  • Equating the SIDM cross‑section required for dwarf cores with the Bullet Cluster limit without accounting for velocity dependence.
  • Stating that Vainshtein screening operates in f(R) gravity; f(R) uses the Chameleon mechanism, not Vainshtein.
  • Neglecting that the Symmetron screening radius depends on the local matter density, leading to environment‑dependent fifth‑force strengths.
  • Overlooking that CMB lensing breaks the geometric degeneracy because it measures the growth of structure, not just the angular diameter distance.

These advanced topics illustrate how precision cosmology combines multiple observables to disentangle fundamental physics from astrophysical systematics.

Type Ia Supernovae as Standardizable Candles and the Discovery of Cosmic Acceleration

Type Ia supernovae (SNe Ia) arise from the thermonuclear disruption of carbon‑oxygen white dwarfs that approach the Chandrasekhar mass (≈1.44 M☉) either by accretion from a companion or by merger. Their peak luminosities show a tight empirical correlation with the light‑curve decline rate: brighter events decline more slowly. This Phillips relation (1993) allows each SN Ia to be standardized to a common absolute magnitude of ≈−19.3 mag in the B band, with an intrinsic scatter of ≈0.12 mag after correction for stretch and colour. In 1998 two independent teams — the Supernova Cosmology Project (Perlmutter et al.) and the High‑Z Supernova Search Team (Riess et al.) — published Hubble diagrams extending to redshift z≈0.9. Both found that distant SNe Ia were systematically fainter than expected in a decelerating Einstein–de Sitter universe, implying an accelerating expansion. The result was quantified by a positive cosmological constant ΩΛ≈0.7 in a flat ΛCDM model. The discovery earned the 2011 Nobel Prize in Physics for Perlmutter, Riess, and Schmidt. Subsequent surveys (ESSENCE, SNLS, Pan‑STARRS, DES, Foundation) have increased the sample to >1500 well‑observed SNe Ia, reducing systematic uncertainties from photometric calibration, host‑galaxy dust, and possible evolution of the Phillips relation. Current joint analyses with CMB and BAO data constrain the dark‑energy equation‑of‑state parameter w to −1.03±0.03, consistent with a cosmological constant.

The Cosmic Distance Ladder and the Calibration of the Hubble Constant

The Hubble constant H₀ is anchored by a hierarchy of distance indicators known as the cosmic distance ladder. The first rung uses geometric parallaxes: Gaia Data Release 3 provides micro‑arcsecond parallaxes for >10⁵ Milky Way Cepheids, reducing the zero‑point uncertainty to ≈0.5 %. The second rung employs Cepheid period‑luminosity (Leavitt) relations calibrated in the Large Magellanic Cloud (distance 49.97 kpc from detached eclipsing binaries) and in NGC 4258 (maser distance 7.6 Mpc). The third rung transfers the Cepheid scale to SNe Ia in the same host galaxies (the SH0ES programme). The SH0ES 2022 analysis yields H₀ = 73.04 ± 1.04 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹, a 1.4 % precision. An independent ladder using the tip of the red‑giant branch (TRGB) in the same SN Ia hosts gives H₀ = 69.8 ± 0.8 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹ (Freedman et al. 2021). The ≈5 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹ tension between the Cepheid and TRGB calibrations, and the ≈5–6 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹ discrepancy with the Planck ΛCDM inference (H₀≈67.4 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹), constitute the “Hubble tension”. Resolving it requires either new physics (early dark energy, neutrino self‑interactions) or unidentified systematics in the ladder (Cepheid metallicity dependence, Gaia zero‑point, SN Ia intrinsic scatter).

Technique
A question asks: “If the Cepheid zero‑point shifts by +0.04 mag, how does the inferred H₀ change? Assume the SN Ia absolute magnitude is fixed.”
A +0.04 mag shift makes Cepheids appear brighter, so the distance to SN Ia hosts decreases by a factor 10^(−0.04/5) ≈ 0.981. Since H₀ ∝ 1/d, H₀ increases by ≈1.9 %. For H₀≈73 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹ this is ≈+1.4 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹.
Exam‑day strategy
Memorise the three‑rung ladder (parallax → Cepheid/TRGB → SN Ia) and the current SH0ES vs. Planck H₀ values. When a question gives a magnitude shift, convert to distance ratio using 10^(Δm/5) and propagate to H₀ linearly. Watch for “inverse” wording: a brighter standard candle implies a larger H₀.

Strong Gravitational Lensing Time Delays as an Independent Probe of H₀

In a strongly lensed quasar system, multiple images traverse different geometric paths and gravitational potentials, producing measurable time delays Δt between flux variations. The time‑delay distance DΔt = (1+zₗ) Dₗ Dₛ / Dₗₛ (where Dₗ, Dₛ, Dₗₛ are angular‑diameter distances to lens, source, and between them) is related to the Fermat potential difference Δφ by Δt = DΔt Δφ / c. Modelling the lens mass distribution (typically a power‑law elliptical profile plus external shear) yields Δφ, and the observed Δt then gives DΔt. Since DΔt ∝ 1/H₀ for fixed lens and source redshifts, a sample of lenses provides a direct H₀ measurement independent of the distance ladder. The H0LiCOW collaboration (2017‑2020) analysed six lenses with high‑resolution HST imaging and COSMOGRAIL light curves, obtaining H₀ = 73.3 ± 1.7 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹. The TDCOSMO programme extended this to 18 lenses, yielding H₀ = 74.2 ± 1.6 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹. The dominant systematic is the mass‑sheet degeneracy: adding a uniform mass sheet κₑₓₜ rescales DΔt by (1−κₑₓₜ)⁻¹. This is broken by stellar kinematics of the lens galaxy (velocity dispersion) and by assuming a prior on the density slope. Recent work incorporates spatially resolved kinematics from integral‑field spectroscopy (e.g., MUSE) to tighten the slope constraint. The lensing H₀ agrees with the Cepheid ladder and exacerbates the tension with Planck.

Primordial Helium Abundance and Its Imprint on the CMB Damping Tail

The primordial helium mass fraction Yₚ is a key prediction of Big Bang nucleosynthesis (BBN) and a free parameter in CMB analyses. Standard BBN with the Planck‑determined baryon density Ω_b h² = 0.0224 predicts Yₚ = 0.2471 ± 0.0002. Helium affects the CMB primarily through its impact on the recombination history: a higher Yₚ reduces the free‑electron fraction at a given redshift, delaying recombination and increasing the sound horizon at last scattering. This shifts the acoustic peaks to slightly larger angular scales (lower ℓ) and, more importantly, enhances Silk damping at high multipoles (ℓ > 1000) because the photon diffusion length grows as the recombination width broadens. Planck 2018 temperature and polarization power spectra constrain Yₚ = 0.245 ± 0.003 (95 % C.L.) when varied jointly with the standard six ΛCDM parameters, consistent with BBN. Allowing Yₚ to vary also weakens the bound on the effective number of neutrino species N_eff, since both parameters alter the damping tail. Future CMB‑S4 measurements of the damping tail to ℓ≈5000 will improve the Yₚ precision to ≈0.001, providing a stringent test of BBN and a probe of exotic energy injection (e.g., decaying particles) that could alter the neutron‑to‑proton ratio before nucleosynthesis.

The Lyman‑α Forest as a Probe of Small‑Scale Matter Power Spectrum

The Lyman‑α forest — the series of absorption lines in quasar spectra caused by neutral hydrogen in the intergalactic medium (IGM) — traces the matter density field at redshifts 2 < z < 5 on comoving scales 0.1–10 h⁻¹ Mpc. The transmitted flux fraction F = exp(−τ) fluctuates with the underlying dark‑matter overdensity δ via the fluctuating Gunn‑Peterson approximation τ ∝ (1+δ)² T⁻⁰·⁷ (1+z)⁴·⁵, where T is the IGM temperature. The one‑dimensional flux power spectrum P_F(k) measured from high‑resolution spectra (e.g., Keck/HIRES, VLT/UVES) and from large‑scale surveys (BOSS, eBOSS, DESI) is related to the linear matter power spectrum P_m(k) through a bias factor and a thermal broadening kernel. Analyses of the BOSS Lyman‑α forest (z≈2.5) constrain the amplitude σ₈ = 0.81 ± 0.02 and the slope n_s = 0.965 ± 0.004, in agreement with Planck. The forest also provides the tightest limits on the sum of neutrino masses Σm_ν < 0.12 eV (95 % C.L.) from the suppression of small‑scale power due to neutrino free‑streaming. Systematic uncertainties include the IGM thermal history (photo‑heating by He II reionization at z≈3), continuum fitting errors, and metal‑line contamination. Joint modelling with hydrodynamical simulations (e.g., Sherwood, Nyx) marginalises over these astrophysical parameters, yielding robust cosmological constraints complementary to CMB and galaxy clustering.

  • Confusing the Phillips relation stretch correction with a simple magnitude offset; the stretch factor rescales the light‑curve time axis, not just the peak.
  • Assuming the Gaia parallax zero‑point is negligible; the 2023 correction of −0.017 mas shifts H₀ by ≈0.5 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹.
  • Neglecting the mass‑sheet degeneracy when interpreting lensing time delays; it rescales H₀ directly unless broken by kinematics.
  • Treating Yₚ as a fixed BBN output in CMB fits; varying Yₚ changes the damping tail and correlates with N_eff.
  • Overlooking that the Lyman‑α flux power spectrum measures the 1D projection of the 3D matter power, requiring a careful bias model.
  • These traps highlight the need for careful reading of cosmology questions.

Primordial Power Spectrum: Scalar Spectral Index, Running, and Tensor‑to‑Scalar Ratio

The primordial curvature perturbation is described by a nearly scale‑invariant power spectrum Pζ(k) = As (k/k*)ns−1 + ½ αs ln(k/k*), where k is the comoving wavenumber, k* = 0.05 Mpc−1 the pivot scale, As the amplitude, ns the scalar spectral index, and αs the running of the index. Planck 2018 temperature‑polarisation data give ns = 0.9649 ± 0.0042 and αs = −0.0045 ± 0.0067 (68 % C.L.), consistent with the slow‑roll prediction αs ≈ 16 ε η − 24 ε2 − 2 ξ2. A detection of non‑zero running would point to features in the inflaton potential or a departure from single‑field slow‑roll.

Tensor perturbations generate a primordial gravitational‑wave background with power spectrum Pt(k) = r As (k/k*)nt, where r is the tensor‑to‑scalar ratio and nt = −2 ε the tensor tilt. The consistency relation r = −8 nt holds for canonical single‑field inflation. Current upper limits from Planck + BICEP/Keck 2018 give r0.05 < 0.036 (95 % C.L.). Translating r into an inflationary energy scale uses V1/4 ≈ (3π2 As r / 2)1/4 MPl ≈ 1.6 × 1016 GeV (r/0.01)1/4. Future CMB‑S4 and LiteBIRD aim for σ(r) ≈ 0.001, probing the GUT‑scale inflation window.

Parameter degeneracies are important: ns correlates with the optical depth τ and the Hubble constant H₀, while r correlates with the lensing amplitude AL and the running αs. Bayesian model comparison (e.g. Bayes factor) is used to assess whether adding running or tensors is warranted by the data. The current evidence favours the base ΛCDM + ns model; extended models are penalised by the Occam factor unless a significant detection emerges.

Cosmic Neutrino Background: Relic Neutrino Detection Prospects and Cosmological Constraints

The cosmic neutrino background (CνB) decoupled at T ≈ 1 MeV (z ≈ 1010) and today has a temperature Tν = (4/11)1/3 TCMB ≈ 1.95 K. Its energy density contributes to the effective number of relativistic species Neff = 3.046 in the standard three‑flavour model (the slight excess over 3 accounts for non‑instantaneous decoupling and QED corrections). Planck 2018 yields Neff = 2.99 ± 0.17 (68 % C.L.), in excellent agreement.

Massive neutrinos suppress the matter power spectrum on scales smaller than the free‑streaming length λfs ≈ 8 Mpc (1 eV / mν). The sum of neutrino masses Σmν is constrained to < 0.12 eV (95 % C.L.) from Planck + BAO, implying a minimum Σmν ≈ 0.06 eV from oscillation data (normal hierarchy) or ≈ 0.10 eV (inverted hierarchy). Future galaxy surveys (DESI, Euclid) combined with CMB‑S4 could reach σ(Σmν) ≈ 0.02 eV, potentially distinguishing the hierarchy.

Direct detection of the CνB is extremely challenging because the neutrino capture cross‑section on β‑decaying nuclei (e.g. 3H → 3He + e− + ν̄e) is ∼10−44 cm2. The PTOLEMY concept aims to capture relic neutrinos on a 100 g tritium target, expecting ∼10 events per year for Σmν ≈ 0.1 eV. A positive signal would provide a direct measurement of the neutrino momentum distribution and a unique probe of the early Universe at T ∼ MeV.

Technique: Inflationary Energy Scale from Tensor‑to‑Scalar Ratio
A CMB experiment reports a tensor‑to‑scalar ratio r = 0.01 at the pivot scale k* = 0.05 Mpc⁻¹. Using the Planck 2018 scalar amplitude As = 2.10 × 10⁻⁹, compute the inflationary energy scale V1/4 in GeV. Assume the reduced Planck mass MPl = 2.435 × 10¹⁸ GeV.
The relation V1/4 = (3π² As r / 2)1/4 MPl. Insert numbers: 3π²/2 ≈ 14.80. Product As r = 2.10 × 10⁻⁹ × 0.01 = 2.10 × 10⁻¹¹. Multiply: 14.80 × 2.10 × 10⁻¹¹ ≈ 3.11 × 10⁻¹⁰. Fourth root: (3.11 × 10⁻¹⁰)1/4 = (3.11)1/4 × 10⁻²·⁵ ≈ 1.33 × 10⁻²·⁵ = 1.33 × 10⁻²·⁵. 10⁻²·⁵ = 10⁻² × 10⁻⁰·⁵ ≈ 0.01 × 0.316 = 3.16 × 10⁻³. So factor ≈ 1.33 × 3.16 × 10⁻³ ≈ 4.20 × 10⁻³. Multiply by MPl: 4.20 × 10⁻³ × 2.435 × 10¹⁸ GeV ≈ 1.02 × 10¹⁶ GeV. Thus V1/4 ≈ 1.0 × 10¹⁶ GeV.
Exam‑Day Strategy
Memorise the key formulae: Pζ(k) parametrisation, consistency relation r = −8 nt, V1/4 scaling with r1/4, free‑streaming length λfs ∝ 1/mν, and Neff = 3.046. When a question gives r, immediately write the V1/4 expression and plug numbers; keep powers of ten separate to avoid arithmetic slips. For neutrino mass limits, remember the hierarchy‑dependent minimum Σmν and the current Planck + BAO bound Σmν < 0.12 eV. Distinguish between cosmological bounds (model‑dependent) and laboratory limits (model‑independent).

Reionization History from CMB Optical Depth and 21‑cm Observations

The Thomson optical depth to reionisation τ quantifies the integrated free‑electron column density: τ = ∫ ne(z) σT c dt/dz dz. Planck 2018 measures τ = 0.054 ± 0.007 (68 % C.L.), implying a mid‑point reionisation redshift zre ≈ 7.7 for a simple tanh model. The large‑scale E‑mode polarisation spectrum (ℓ < 10) is the primary CMB probe of τ; its amplitude scales as τ². Degeneracies with the scalar amplitude As and the spectral index ns are broken by the high‑ℓ temperature and polarisation data.

21‑cm intensity mapping of the neutral hydrogen spin‑flip transition provides a tomographic view of the ionisation field. The differential brightness temperature relative to the CMB is δTb ≈ 27 xHI (1+δ) (1+z)/101/2 (0.15/Ωmh²)1/2 mK, where xHI is the neutral fraction. During the Epoch of Reionisation (EoR) the 21‑cm power spectrum evolves from large‑scale fluctuations driven by density and ionisation bubbles to a post‑reionisation floor set by the residual neutral islands. Current upper limits from HERA (z ≈ 7.9) and LOFAR (z ≈ 9.1) constrain the 21‑cm power spectrum amplitude Δ²(k) < few × 10³ mK² at k ≈ 0.1 h Mpc⁻¹, beginning to exclude extremely cold IGM scenarios.

Joint analysis of τ and 21‑cm data breaks the degeneracy between the timing and duration of reionisation. A high τ with a short duration requires early, efficient photon production (e.g. faint galaxies with high escape fractions). Conversely, a low τ with extended 21‑cm absorption signals a late, gradual reionisation driven by low‑mass galaxies. Future SKA‑Low observations will measure the 21‑cm power spectrum to Δ² ≈ 10 mK², enabling reconstruction of the ionisation history xHI(z) and the typical bubble size distribution.

Galaxy Bias and Halo Occupation Distribution in Large‑Scale Structure Analyses

Galaxies are biased tracers of the underlying matter density field: δg(k) = b1 δm(k) + ½ b2 δm²(k) + …. On large scales the linear bias b1 is scale‑independent; typical values are b1 ≈ 1–2 for L* galaxies at z ≈ 0.5. The Halo Occupation Distribution (HOD) models the mean number of galaxies in a halo of mass M as ⟨Ng|M⟩ = ⟨Ncen|M⟩ + ⟨Nsat|M⟩, with a central step function ⟨Ncen⟩ = ½[1+erf((log M−log Mmin)/σlog M)] and a satellite power law ⟨Nsat⟩ = ((M−M0)/M1)α for M > M0. The five parameters (Mmin, σlog M, M0, M1, α) are constrained by the projected two‑point correlation function wp(rp) and the galaxy number density ng.

Redshift‑space distortions (RSD) introduce an anisotropic clustering signal parameterised by the growth rate fσ₈. The Kaiser formula for the monopole and quadrupole of the redshift‑space power spectrum includes the linear bias b1 and the velocity dispersion σv (Finger‑of‑God effect). Modern analyses (e.g. BOSS DR12, eBOSS) fit the full anisotropic power spectrum Pg(k,μ) with a perturbation‑theory model (TNS or EFT) that incorporates b1, b2, bs² (tidal bias), and counterterms. The resulting fσ₈ measurements at multiple redshifts provide a stringent test of General Relativity and the ΛCDM growth history.

Assembly bias — the dependence of halo clustering on secondary properties (concentration, formation time) at fixed mass — can induce scale‑dependent galaxy bias if the HOD depends on those properties. Ignoring assembly bias leads to biased cosmological parameter inference, particularly for σ₈ and ns. Recent hydrodynamical simulations (IllustrisTNG, SIMBA) are used to calibrate HOD extensions that include a dependence on halo concentration, reducing systematic errors to < 1 % on fσ₈ for upcoming DESI and Euclid surveys.

CMB Component Separation Algorithms: Commander, NILC, SMICA, and SEVEM

Accurate cosmological inference requires separating the CMB from Galactic foregrounds (synchrotron, free‑free, thermal dust, anomalous microwave emission) and extragalactic contaminants (CIB, tSZ, kSZ). Four widely used component‑separation pipelines on Planck data are Commander, NILC, SMICA, and SEVEM. Commander adopts a parametric Bayesian approach: it fits a physical model for each foreground component (e.g. power‑law synchrotron, modified blackbody dust) pixel‑by‑pixel using Gibbs sampling, yielding a CMB map with a full posterior covariance. NILC (Needlet Internal Linear Combination) works in harmonic space with needlet localisation; it constructs minimum‑variance linear combinations of frequency maps in each needlet domain, assuming only that foregrounds are uncorrelated with the CMB. SMICA (Spectral Matching Independent Component Analysis) performs a parametric spectral fit in harmonic space, modelling foreground spectra as power laws or modified blackbodies and solving for the mixing matrix via maximum likelihood. SEVEM (Template fitting) uses external foreground templates (e.g. 408 MHz synchrotron map, Planck 857 GHz dust map) and fits coefficients in pixel space after masking the Galactic plane.

All four methods produce consistent CMB temperature and polarisation power spectra at the level of ΔCℓ/Cℓ < 0.1 % for ℓ < 2000, but differences appear in the high‑ℓ tail and in the residual foreground bias on the lensing potential. The Planck 2018 likelihood adopts a consensus approach: the baseline likelihood uses the Commander map for low‑ℓ (ℓ < 30) and a combination of NILC/SMICA/SEVEM for high‑ℓ, with a foreground marginalisation that propagates the inter‑method scatter into the covariance. For future experiments (CMB‑S4, LiteBIRD), component separation will need to handle higher sensitivity, wider frequency coverage, and polarisation‑specific systematics (e.g. bandpass mismatch, polarised foreground spectral complexity). Machine‑learning based methods (e.g. deep‑learning denoisers, variational autoencoders) are being explored to complement traditional pipelines.

  • Confusing the scalar spectral index ns with the tensor tilt nt; they have opposite signs in the consistency relation.
  • Assuming the cosmic neutrino background temperature equals the CMB temperature; it is lower by (4/11)1/3.
  • Treating the optical depth τ as a direct measure of the reionisation redshift without accounting for the duration parameter Δz.
  • Neglecting the scale‑dependence of galaxy bias on quasi‑linear scales; using a single b1 for all k leads to biased fσ₈.
  • Believing that all component‑separation methods give identical foreground residuals; their different assumptions produce distinct bias patterns that must be marginalised.
  • Overlooking the impact of assembly bias on HOD inference; ignoring it can shift σ₈ by > 1 %.
  • Using the simple Kaiser formula for RSD on small scales where Finger‑of‑God damping dominates; a full perturbation‑theory model is required.

CMB Polarization Foregrounds: Synchrotron and Dust Modeling

Accurate measurement of the primordial B‑mode signal requires precise modeling of Galactic polarized foregrounds. The two dominant components are synchrotron emission from relativistic electrons spiraling in the Galactic magnetic field and thermal dust emission from aligned aspherical grains. Synchrotron polarization follows a power‑law frequency dependence I ∝ νβs with a spectral index βs ≈ −3.0 to −3.2 in intensity; the polarized spectral index βspol is typically steeper by Δβ ≈ −0.1 to −0.2 due to energy‑dependent electron propagation. Thermal dust polarization is well described by a modified blackbody I ∝ νβd Bν(Td) with βd ≈ 1.5–1.6 and dust temperature Td ≈ 19–20 K. Both components exhibit significant spatial variation: βs steepens toward the Galactic plane, while βd and Td anti‑correlate in molecular clouds.

Component‑separation pipelines exploit multi‑frequency data (e.g., Planck 30–353 GHz, WMAP 23–94 GHz, and ground‑based experiments such as BICEP/Keck at 95/150 GHz). Parametric methods (Commander) fit pixel‑by‑pixel spectral parameters with priors derived from low‑frequency radio surveys (Haslam 408 MHz, S‑PASS 2.3 GHz) and high‑frequency dust maps (Planck 857 GHz). Blind methods (NILC, SMICA) assume only statistical independence between CMB and foregrounds, constructing minimum‑variance linear combinations in needlet or harmonic space. The residual foreground bias on the tensor‑to‑scalar ratio r is quantified by simulating realistic sky models (e.g., PySM3) and propagating through the analysis pipeline; current upper limits r < 0.032 (95 % CL) from BICEP/Keck 2018+Planck already marginalize over synchrotron and dust spectral‑index uncertainties.

Key systematic effects include: (1) frequency decorrelation of dust polarization due to varying grain alignment efficiency along the line of sight, which can mimic a scale‑dependent B‑mode spectrum; (2) synchrotron curvature (deviation from a pure power law) at low frequencies, relevant for future low‑frequency missions (e.g., LiteBIRD 40–400 GHz); (3) anomalous microwave emission (AME) polarization, currently constrained to < 1 % of total intensity but potentially non‑negligible for r ∼ 10−3 targets. Ongoing efforts combine high‑resolution dust polarization maps from SO and CMB‑S4 with low‑frequency radio surveys to build a joint likelihood for βs, βd, Td, and the dust‑synchrotron correlation coefficient ρsd.

Weak Lensing Tomography and Photometric Redshift Calibration

Cosmic shear tomography divides a galaxy sample into multiple redshift bins and measures the angular shear power spectra Cℓij between bins i and j. The signal depends on the matter power spectrum Pm(k,z) and the lensing efficiency kernel, which peaks at roughly half the source redshift. Photometric redshift (photo‑z) errors broaden the true redshift distribution ni(z) of each tomographic bin, causing leakage of power between bins and biasing cosmological parameters, especially σ8 and Ωm. The bias can be expressed as Δp ≈ F−1 ∂C/∂p ⋅ Cov−1 ⋅ ΔC, where ΔC encodes the shift in predicted spectra due to photo‑z mis‑estimation.

Calibration strategies fall into two categories: (1) spectroscopic cross‑matching with deep surveys (e.g., DEEP2, VVDS, zCOSMOS) to directly estimate the conditional probability p(zspec|zphot) and its moments; (2) clustering‑based redshifts (cross‑correlation with a spectroscopic reference sample) that measure the angular cross‑correlation wθ(θ) between photometric and spectroscopic galaxies, yielding the mean redshift ⟨z⟩ of each bin without requiring individual secure redshifts. The latter is powerful for faint galaxies beyond spectroscopic limits but assumes linear bias and negligible magnification bias.

Current Stage‑III surveys (DES‑Y3, HSC‑Y3, KiDS‑1000) achieve photo‑z scatter σz/(1+z) ≈ 0.02–0.03 and outlier fractions < 5 % after applying self‑organizing map (SOM) or machine‑learning (e.g., TPZ, DNF) calibrations. Stage‑IV surveys (LSST, Euclid, Roman) target σz/(1+z) ≤ 0.01 and outlier fractions < 1 % using a combination of deep spectroscopic training sets (e.g., PFS, WEAVE) and clustering redshifts. Residual systematic uncertainties are propagated via nuisance parameters (shift Δzi, stretch σz,i) in the cosmological likelihood, with priors derived from the calibration data. The impact on the dark‑energy figure of merit (FoM) is roughly proportional to (σz/0.01)2, making photo‑z control a primary driver of survey design.

Foreground cleaning bias in CMB polarization
A component‑separation analysis assumes a constant dust spectral index βd = 1.55 across the sky. Simulations show the true βd varies with a standard deviation of 0.1. If the analysis ignores this variation, estimate the induced bias on the tensor‑to‑scalar ratio r for a survey targeting r = 0.001, given that the dust B‑mode power at ℓ = 80 scales as DℓBB ∝ ν2βd and the experiment observes at 150 GHz with a dust amplitude Ad = 5 µK2 at 353 GHz.
The dust B‑mode power at 150 GHz is D80 = Ad (150/353)2βd. For βd = 1.55, (150/353)3.10 ≈ 0.018, giving D80 ≈ 0.09 µK2. A variation Δβ = 0.1 changes the exponent by 0.2, so the fractional change in power is ≈ 2 ln(150/353) Δβ ≈ 2 (−0.86) (0.1) ≈ −0.17. The rms bias on D80 is 0.17 × 0.09 µK2 ≈ 0.015 µK2. The primordial B‑mode power for r = 0.001 at ℓ = 80 is ≈ 0.01 µK2. Thus the dust spectral‑index variation induces a bias comparable to the target signal, Δr ≈ 0.0015. Proper marginalization over βd spatial variation is essential.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for the impact of a foreground parameter variation on r, first write the scaling relation of the foreground power with frequency and spectral index. Compute the derivative of the power with respect to the parameter, multiply by the parameter uncertainty, and compare the resulting power shift to the primordial B‑mode power for the target r. This dimensional‑analysis approach yields the bias order of magnitude without full simulation.

Baryon Acoustic Oscillation Reconstruction Techniques and Their Impact on Distance Measurements

Baryon acoustic oscillations (BAO) provide a standard ruler of comoving length rs ≈ 147 Mpc, imprinted in the correlation function ξ(s) of galaxies and quasars. Non‑linear structure formation, redshift‑space distortions, and galaxy bias smear the acoustic peak, degrading distance precision. Reconstruction reverses large‑scale bulk flows by estimating the displacement field ψ from the observed density contrast δg using linear theory: ψ(k) = −i k/k2 δg(k)/b1. Shifting galaxies by −ψ sharpens the BAO feature, reducing the isotropic dilation parameter α error by 30–50 % for typical survey volumes (e.g., BOSS DR12, eBOSS).

Two main reconstruction algorithms are used: (1) Standard reconstruction (Eisenstein et al. 2007) applies a smoothing scale R ≈ 15 Mpc/h to the density field before computing ψ, then shifts both data and random catalogs. (2) Iterative reconstruction (e.g., Schmittfull et al. 2017) repeats the displacement estimation on the shifted field, converging after 3–4 iterations and recovering additional information from higher‑order correlations. Both methods assume a fiducial cosmology for the linear growth factor f and bias b1; mismatches introduce a small systematic shift in α that is calibrated with mock catalogs.

The reconstructed correlation function is fitted with a template ξmodel(s) = B2 ξlin(s/α) ⊗ G(Σnl) + A(s), where B is the broadband amplitude, G is a Gaussian damping with width Σnl (≈ 5 Mpc/h after reconstruction), and A(s) is a polynomial marginalizing over broadband shape. The anisotropic fit separates α∥ (radial) and α⊥ (transverse), yielding H(z) and DA(z) respectively. Recent analyses (eBOSS LRG, ELG, QSO) achieve 1–2 % precision on DA and 2–3 % on H(z) at effective redshifts 0.7–1.5. Systematic error budgets allocate ≤ 0.3 % to reconstruction algorithm choice, ≤ 0.2 % to fiducial cosmology dependence, and ≤ 0.1 % to redshift‑space distortion modeling after reconstruction.

Primordial Black Hole Constraints from Microlensing and Accretion

Primordial black holes (PBHs) formed from large density fluctuations in the early universe remain a viable dark‑matter candidate in mass windows not excluded by observations. Microlensing surveys (MACHO, EROS, OGLE, Subaru HSC) constrain the PBH fraction fPBH = ΩPBH/ΩDM in the range 10−7–10 M⊙. The optical depth τ for microlensing of stars in the Magellanic Clouds or M31 scales as τ ∝ fPBH (M/ M⊙)1/2. EROS‑2 and MACHO combined exclude fPBH > 0.1 for 0.1–1 M⊙, while OGLE’s 5‑year Galactic bulge data push the limit to fPBH < 0.01 at 10 M⊙. Subaru HSC’s high‑cadence monitoring of M31 extends constraints down to 10−5 M⊙ (asteroid masses), reaching fPBH < 0.1.

Accretion onto PBHs in the early universe produces X‑ray and radio emission that alters the thermal history and leaves imprints on the CMB. The accretion luminosity L ∝ Ṁ c2 with Bondi‑Hoyle rate Ṁ ∝ ρb M2 / v3, where ρb is the baryon density and v the relative velocity. Energy deposition ionizes and heats the intergalactic medium, modifying the recombination history and the CMB anisotropy damping tail. Planck 2018 data constrain fPBH < 10−3 for M ≈ 10–100 M⊙ assuming spherical accretion with radiative efficiency η = 0.1. More conservative treatments (including feedback, relative velocities, and non‑spherical geometry) weaken the bound to fPBH < 0.1 in the same mass range.

Gravitational‑wave observations of binary black‑hole mergers (LIGO/Virgo/KAGRA) provide an independent probe. The merger rate density ℛ ∝ fPBH2 M−32/37 for PBH binaries formed in the early universe. Current O3 data constrain fPBH < 0.1 for 10–100 M⊙ under standard clustering assumptions. Future detectors (Einstein Telescope, Cosmic Explorer) will improve sensitivity by an order of magnitude, potentially closing the remaining asteroid‑mass window (10−16–10−12 M⊙) where PBHs could constitute all dark matter.

  • Assuming a single dust spectral index for the whole sky; spatial variation of βd biases r at the level of the target sensitivity.
  • Neglecting photo‑z outlier fractions when propagating cosmological parameter errors; even a 1 % outlier tail can shift σ8 by > 0.5 %.
  • Using the pre‑reconstruction BAO peak position as the standard ruler; the non‑linear shift must be calibrated with mocks.
  • Treating microlensing optical depth limits as mass‑independent; the τ ∝ M1/2 scaling changes exclusion contours dramatically.
  • Ignoring the dependence of CMB accretion bounds on the assumed radiative efficiency and relative velocity distribution.
  • Confusing the PBH merger rate scaling with the primordial binary formation channel; the rate ∝ fPBH2 only for early‑universe binaries.
  • Overlooking the correlation between synchrotron and dust polarization (ρsd) in component‑separation likelihoods; it can mimic a primordial B‑mode signal.

These advanced topics illustrate how instrumental systematics, astrophysical modeling, and statistical methodology intertwine in modern cosmological inference.

The Rees‑Sciama Effect and Non‑Linear ISW Contributions

The Rees‑Sciama effect is the non‑linear counterpart of the integrated Sachs‑Wolfe (ISW) effect. While the linear ISW arises from the decay of gravitational potentials during the dark‑energy‑dominated era, the Rees‑Sciama effect originates from the time evolution of potentials in the non‑linear regime of structure formation — collapsing clusters, expanding voids, and merging halos. Rees and Sciama (1968) first showed that photons traversing a changing potential well acquire a net energy shift proportional to the time derivative of the potential along the line of sight. In the linear regime this derivative is set by the background expansion; in the non‑linear regime it is driven by the growth of density perturbations themselves.

The temperature anisotropy from the Rees‑Sciama effect can be written as a line‑of‑sight integral of the potential time derivative, ΔT/T = 2 ∫ (∂Φ/∂η) dη, where η is conformal time. Because the non‑linear potential evolves on the dynamical time of the structure, the effect is concentrated at angular scales corresponding to the size of massive clusters and superclusters, roughly multipoles ℓ ∼ 10–100. Its power spectrum is subdominant to the primary CMB anisotropies and to the linear ISW at low ℓ, but it becomes a non‑negligible contaminant for primordial non‑Gaussianity searches. The bispectrum generated by the Rees‑Sciama effect peaks for squeezed triangle configurations and mimics a local‑type f_NL of order unity, well below current Planck limits (f_NL^local = −0.9 ± 5.1) but relevant for future experiments targeting σ(f_NL) ∼ 1.

Observationally, the Rees‑Sciama contribution is constrained through cross‑correlation of CMB maps with large‑scale structure tracers. Planck 2015 and 2018 analyses cross‑correlated the SMICA temperature map with the WISE‑SuperCOSMOS galaxy catalogue and with the Planck lensing potential, placing upper limits on the non‑linear ISW power at the level of a few percent of the linear ISW signal. The effect also enters the CMB lensing potential power spectrum through the correlation between the lensing potential and the time‑derivative of the potential, a term that is included in the full Boltzmann codes (e.g., CLASS, CAMB) when computing the lensed CMB spectra to high precision.

From a theoretical standpoint, the Rees‑Sciama effect is a second‑order perturbation theory result. It can be computed using the non‑linear matter power spectrum from N‑body simulations or from fitting functions such as HALOFIT. The amplitude scales roughly as σ84, making it a sensitive probe of the amplitude of matter fluctuations. However, the effect is degenerate with the kinetic Sunyaev‑Zel'dovich (kSZ) signal from the same structures, and disentangling them requires multi‑frequency data or statistical separation using the distinct angular dependence of the two signals.

In summary, the Rees‑Sciama effect is a small but well‑understood non‑linear correction to the CMB temperature anisotropies. It must be modelled accurately for high‑precision cosmology, particularly for future CMB‑S4 and LiteBIRD analyses that aim to extract primordial non‑Gaussianity and the neutrino mass from the damping tail and lensing reconstruction.

The Ostriker‑Vishniac Effect: Doppler Signals from Patchy Reionization

The Ostriker‑Vishniac (OV) effect is a second‑order Doppler anisotropy produced when CMB photons scatter off free electrons in ionized bubbles during the epoch of reionization. First described by Ostriker and Vishniac (1986) and later refined by Vishniac (1987), the effect arises because the bulk velocity of the ionized gas is correlated with the density field; photons last‑scatter in moving electron clouds and acquire a temperature shift ΔT/T = −∫ (v·n) σ_T n_e a dη, where v is the peculiar velocity, n the line‑of‑sight direction, σ_T the Thomson cross‑section, n_e the electron density, and a the scale factor. Unlike the primary Doppler effect at recombination, the OV effect is sourced by the patchy, inhomogeneous ionization field, which modulates the scattering optical depth.

The angular power spectrum of the OV effect peaks at multipoles ℓ ∼ 3000–10000, corresponding to angular scales of a few arcminutes. Its amplitude depends critically on the reionization history: the duration Δz, the typical bubble size R_b, and the mean ionized fraction x_e(z). For a fiducial reionization model with midpoint z_re ∼ 8 and Δz ∼ 1, the OV contribution to the temperature power spectrum is of order 1–5 µK2 at ℓ ∼ 3000, comparable to the kinetic Sunyaev‑Zel'dovich (kSZ) signal from the same epoch but with a different scale dependence. The OV effect is also a source of non‑Gaussianity; its bispectrum has a characteristic shape that peaks for equilateral triangles, providing a potential discriminant against primordial non‑Gaussianity.

Current observational limits come from high‑resolution CMB experiments such as the Atacama Cosmology Telescope (ACT) and the South Pole Telescope (SPT). ACTPol and SPT‑3G have measured the temperature power spectrum to ℓ > 10000 and placed upper bounds on the patchy kSZ/OV power at D_ℓ = ℓ(ℓ+1)C_ℓ/2π < 2–3 µK2 at ℓ = 3000 (95% CL). These limits translate into constraints on the reionization duration Δz < 2–3 and on the typical bubble size R_b > 5–10 Mpc (comoving). Cross‑correlation of the CMB temperature map with 21‑cm intensity maps from HERA or the Square Kilometre Array (SKA) will dramatically improve these constraints by directly probing the ionization morphology.

The OV effect is often grouped with the homogeneous kSZ (from the bulk flow of fully ionized gas after reionization) under the term “patchy kSZ”. The distinction is that the homogeneous kSZ traces the velocity field at low redshift (z < 1) and peaks at ℓ ∼ 3000–5000, while the OV effect traces the velocity field at z ∼ 6–10 and peaks at slightly higher ℓ. Component separation using multi‑frequency data is not possible because both signals are frequency‑independent; statistical separation relies on their different redshift kernels and on cross‑correlation with external tracers such as galaxy surveys or 21‑cm maps.

Future CMB‑S4 measurements, combined with 21‑cm tomography, will enable a tomographic reconstruction of the reionization history through the OV effect. The detection of the OV bispectrum would provide a direct probe of the three‑dimensional ionization field, complementing the 21‑cm power spectrum and offering a unique window on the first galaxies and the sources of reionization.

Order‑of‑Magnitude Estimation
Estimate the typical temperature fluctuation ΔT/T from the Ostriker‑Vishniac effect for a reionization bubble of comoving radius 10 Mpc at z = 8, assuming a peculiar velocity of 300 km/s and an ionized fraction x_e = 0.5. Use the Thomson optical depth through the bubble τ_b ≈ σ_T n_e x_e R_b, with n_e ≈ 2×10^−7 cm^−3 (the mean electron density at z = 8). Express your answer in µK.
The optical depth through the bubble is τ_b = σ_T n_e x_e R_b. σ_T = 6.65×10^−25 cm2. n_e = 2×10^−7 cm^−3. x_e = 0.5. R_b = 10 Mpc = 3.09×1025 cm. τ_b = (6.65×10^−25)(2×10^−7)(0.5)(3.09×1025) ≈ 2.05×10^−6. The Doppler shift is ΔT/T ≈ τ_b (v/c) with v = 300 km/s = 3×107 cm/s, c = 3×1010 cm/s, so v/c = 10^−3. Thus ΔT/T ≈ 2.05×10^−6 × 10^−3 = 2.05×10^−9. The CMB temperature T0 = 2.725 K = 2.725×106 µK. ΔT = (ΔT/T) T0 ≈ 2.05×10^−9 × 2.725×106 µK ≈ 5.6×10^−3 µK. This is the fluctuation from a single bubble; the observed OV power spectrum sums over many bubbles, yielding µK2 level power at ℓ ∼ 3000.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for an order‑of‑magnitude estimate of a second‑order CMB effect, identify the relevant optical depth and velocity factor, compute τ × (v/c), and multiply by the CMB temperature in µK. Remember that the OV effect is frequency‑independent and peaks at ℓ ∼ 3000–10000; the homogeneous kSZ peaks at slightly lower ℓ. Use the scaling τ ∝ x_e (1+z)2 R_b to check how the signal changes with reionization parameters.

Moving Lens Effect: CMB Lensing by Transverse Structure Motions

The moving lens effect describes the time‑dependent gravitational deflection of CMB photons by a massive structure moving transverse to the line of sight. As a cluster or filament moves with transverse velocity v_⊥, the gravitational potential at a fixed angular position on the sky changes, imparting a dipole‑like temperature pattern and a distinctive polarization signature. The effect was first discussed by Gurvits and Mitrofanov (1986) and later quantified by Birkinshaw and Gull (1983) in the context of the Sachs‑Wolfe formula for a moving potential. The temperature shift for a photon passing a moving point mass M at impact parameter b is ΔT/T ≈ (4GM/c2) (v_⊥/c) (b/|b|2) · n, where n is the direction of motion projected on the sky. This shift is frequency‑independent, unlike the kinetic Sunyaev‑Zel'dovich effect, and its amplitude is set by the ratio v_⊥/c ∼ 10^−3 for typical cluster velocities of 1000 km/s.

The moving lens effect produces a temperature dipole centred on the lens, with an amplitude of order 10–100 nK for a massive cluster (M ∼ 1015 M_⊙) at z ∼ 0.5. The associated polarization pattern is a curl‑type (B‑mode) distortion with a similar amplitude. Because the effect is proportional to the transverse velocity, it provides a direct probe of the peculiar velocity field on large scales, complementary to the line‑of‑sight velocities measured by the kSZ effect. The moving lens signal is also correlated with the CMB lensing convergence field, allowing a joint analysis that can separate the moving lens contribution from the primary CMB and from the kSZ.

Observational detection of the moving lens effect is challenging due to its small amplitude. Current CMB experiments (Planck, ACT, SPT) lack the sensitivity and resolution to detect individual moving lenses. However, statistical detection via cross‑correlation with galaxy surveys is feasible. The cross‑power spectrum between the CMB temperature map and a galaxy density map weighted by the transverse velocity field (reconstructed from redshift‑space distortions) yields a signal‑to‑noise ratio of order unity for a Stage‑4 CMB experiment combined with a spectroscopic survey like DESI or Euclid. The moving lens effect also contributes to the CMB lensing potential power spectrum at the percent level, an effect that must be included in high‑precision lensing reconstruction for delensing primordial B‑modes.

From a theoretical perspective, the moving lens effect is a first‑order effect in v_⊥/c but second order in the gravitational potential (since the deflection angle itself is first order in Φ/c2). It can be incorporated into Boltzmann codes by adding a time‑dependent lensing potential term. The effect is distinct from the Rees‑Sciama effect, which arises from the time evolution of the potential in the rest frame of the structure, whereas the moving lens effect arises from the motion of the structure across the line of sight. Both are non‑linear corrections to the linear ISW effect, but they have different angular signatures and redshift dependencies.

Future CMB‑S4 and Simons Observatory data, combined with high‑density galaxy surveys, will enable the first statistical detection of the moving lens effect. This will open a new window on the transverse velocity field of the cosmic web, providing a test of general relativity on large scales and a novel probe of the growth of structure.

CMB Lensing Reconstruction with Polarization‑Only Quadratic Estimators

CMB lensing reconstruction traditionally uses quadratic combinations of temperature and polarization fields (TT, TE, EE, EB, TB) to build an estimator for the lensing potential φ. The Hu‑Okamoto (2002) formalism shows that the minimum‑variance estimator weights each quadratic pair by its inverse noise. At low multipoles (L < 100) the temperature field dominates the signal‑to‑noise because of its high signal‑to‑noise and large sky coverage. However, temperature maps are contaminated by foregrounds (tSZ, kSZ, CIB, radio sources) and by instrumental systematics that can bias the lensing potential. Polarization‑only estimators (EE, EB) avoid these contaminants entirely, since the primary CMB polarization is free of tSZ and kSZ (which are unpolarized at leading order) and has different foreground spectral behaviour.

The reconstruction noise N_L^φφ for polarization‑only estimators is higher than for the minimum‑variance combination at low L, but it becomes competitive at L > 200 where the polarization signal‑to‑noise improves. For a CMB‑S4‑like experiment with 1 µK‑arcmin polarization noise and 1 arcmin beam, the EE estimator achieves N_L^φφ ≈ 10^−8 at L ∼ 500, while the EB estimator is slightly noisier but has the advantage of being immune to E‑to‑B leakage from miscalibration. The polarization‑only reconstruction is therefore the method of choice for delensing the large‑scale B‑mode signal, where any bias from temperature foregrounds would mimic a primordial gravitational‑wave signal.

Current analyses illustrate the trade‑offs. Planck 2018 lensing used the minimum‑variance combination (including TT) to achieve the highest overall signal‑to‑noise, but the polarization‑only (EE+EB) reconstruction was used as a systematic check and showed consistency within errors. The ACTPol and SPT‑3G teams have published polarization‑only lensing reconstructions, demonstrating that the EB estimator can recover the lensing potential with a signal‑to‑noise of ∼ 10 on the power spectrum amplitude. The Simons Observatory will rely heavily on polarization‑only estimators for its delensing pipeline, using the EE estimator for the lensing potential and the EB estimator as a null test for systematics.

Beyond the quadratic estimator, iterative maximum‑likelihood methods (e.g., the Millea‑Anderes‑Wandelt algorithm) can achieve the Cramér‑Rao bound and are naturally formulated in polarization‑only space. These methods are computationally more demanding but avoid the Gaussian bias (N^(0)) and the higher‑order biases (N^(1), N^(2)) that plague quadratic estimators. For future experiments targeting r < 0.001, the control of lensing reconstruction biases at the 0.1% level will require either polarization‑only iterative reconstruction or a careful combination of quadratic estimators with extensive bias‑hardening.

In summary, polarization‑only quadratic estimators provide a clean, foreground‑robust route to CMB lensing reconstruction. Their higher noise at low L is mitigated by the fact that delensing of the reionization bump (L < 10) relies on large‑scale lensing modes that can be supplemented by external tracers (CIB, galaxy surveys). The development of bias‑hardened polarization‑only estimators and their validation on simulated and real data is a critical path for the next generation of CMB polarization experiments.

The Cosmic Optical Background and Its Constraints on Reionization

The cosmic optical background (COB) is the integrated flux from all stellar and galactic emission over cosmic history, observed at optical and near‑infrared wavelengths (0.3–5 µm). It encodes the total star formation activity, the initial mass function, and the escape fraction of ionizing photons, making it a powerful complement to CMB and 21‑cm probes of reionization. The COB intensity I_ν is related to the comoving emissivity ε_ν(z) by I_ν = (c/4π) ∫ ε_ν(z) (dt/dz) dz / (1+z). Because the emissivity is dominated by stars, the COB directly measures the stellar mass density ρ_* and the production rate of hydrogen‑ionizing photons N_ion, which must satisfy the photon budget for reionization: ∫ N_ion f_esc dt ≥ n_H (1 + n_rec), where f_esc is the escape fraction and n_rec the number of recombinations per hydrogen atom.

Measurements of the COB have a long history of controversy. Ground‑based and HST measurements suffered from zodiacal light foregrounds and scattered light systematics, leading to widely discrepant results ranging from 10 to 100 nW m^−2 sr^−1 at 0.5 µm. The New Horizons spacecraft, operating beyond the zodiacal dust cloud, provided the cleanest measurement to date: I_ν ≈ 11.2 ± 1.5 nW m^−2 sr^−1 at 0.6 µm (LORRI band). This value is significantly lower than earlier HST‑based estimates and implies a lower integrated star formation rate density at z > 2. When combined with galaxy counts from deep HST and JWST surveys, the New Horizons COB leaves little room for a large population of faint, undetected galaxies, constraining the faint‑end slope of the UV luminosity function and the total ionizing photon budget.

The COB also constrains the Thomson optical depth τ measured by Planck (τ = 0.054 ± 0.007). A low COB intensity favours a late, rapid reionization (z_re ∼ 7–8) with a high escape fraction f_esc ∼ 0.1–0.2, while a higher COB would allow an extended reionization with lower f_esc. Recent joint analyses of the COB, the CMB τ, and the UV luminosity function from JWST indicate that galaxies with M_UV < −13 can provide sufficient photons for reionization only if f_esc > 0.15, a value in tension with direct measurements at lower redshift. This tension highlights the importance of accurate COB measurements for breaking degeneracies in reionization models.

Future COB measurements will come from the SPHEREx mission (0.75–5 µm spectroscopy over the full sky) and from the CIBER‑2 sounding rocket experiment. SPHEREx will map the COB fluctuations (anisotropies) at the 1 nW m^−2 sr^−1 level, providing a tomographic probe of the emission history through cross‑correlation with galaxy surveys and 21‑cm intensity maps. The COB anisotropy power spectrum at angular scales ℓ ∼ 1000–10000 is sensitive to the clustering of faint galaxies during reionization and can distinguish between different reionization topologies (inside‑out vs. outside‑in).

In conclusion, the cosmic optical background is a unique integral constraint on the star formation history and the ionizing photon budget. Its recent low measurement by New Horizons sharpens the photon‑counting problem for reionization and motivates deeper galaxy surveys and improved escape fraction measurements. The synergy between COB, CMB τ, 21‑cm, and JWST galaxy counts will ultimately deliver a self‑consistent picture of the epoch of reionization.

  • Confusing the Rees‑Sciama effect with the linear ISW effect; the former is non‑linear, scales as σ84, and peaks at higher multipoles.
  • Assuming the Ostriker‑Vishniac effect is frequency‑dependent; it is a pure Doppler signal and therefore frequency‑independent, like the kSZ.
  • Neglecting the transverse velocity in moving lens estimates; the effect is proportional to v_⊥/c, not the line‑of‑sight velocity.
  • Using temperature‑based lensing reconstruction for delensing primordial B‑modes without checking polarization‑only consistency; temperature foregrounds can bias the lensing potential at the level of r ∼ 0.001.
  • Treating the cosmic optical background as a direct measure of the ionizing emissivity; the COB traces non‑ionizing UV/optical light, and conversion to ionizing photons requires assumptions about the stellar SED and escape fraction.
  • Overlooking the fact that the OV bispectrum peaks for equilateral triangles, while the Rees‑Sciama bispectrum peaks for squeezed triangles; this distinction is crucial for non‑Gaussianity model discrimination.
  • Ignoring the redshift dependence of the moving lens kernel; it peaks at z ∼ 0.5, whereas the kSZ kernel peaks at z ∼ 1, affecting cross‑correlation strategies.
The advanced topics above illustrate how second‑order CMB effects, lensing reconstruction choices, and background light measurements intertwine to shape modern cosmological inference.

CMB Lensing Reconstruction with Temperature‑Only Quadratic Estimators

The temperature‑only quadratic estimator remains the workhorse for lensing reconstruction in high‑resolution, temperature‑dominated experiments such as the Atacama Cosmology Telescope (ACT) and the South Pole Telescope (SPT). The lensed temperature field T(n̂) is expanded to first order in the lensing potential ϕ: T(n̂) ≈ Tunl(n̂) + ∇Tunl·∇ϕ. In harmonic space this coupling generates off‑diagonal correlations ⟨al1 m1 al2 m2⟩ proportional to the lensing potential multipoles ϕLM. A quadratic combination of temperature multipoles weighted by the inverse of the unlensed temperature power spectrum ClTT yields an unbiased estimator for ϕLM:

ϕ̂LM = AL Σl1 m1 l2 m2 (−1)M ( l1 l2 L ; m1 m2 −M ) fl1 l2 LTT al1 m1 al2 m2 / (Cl1TT Cl2TT), where the weight function fl1 l2 LTT encodes the geometric coupling and the response of the temperature field to a gradient of the potential. The normalization AL is fixed by requiring ⟨ϕ̂LM⟩ = ϕLM for an isotropic Gaussian unlensed sky, giving AL−1 = (2L+1)−1 Σl1 l2 (2l1+1)(2l2+1) (fl1 l2 LTT)2 / (Cl1TT Cl2TT).

The dominant systematic is the Gaussian (N0) bias, which arises from the disconnected four‑point function of the unlensed temperature field. It must be subtracted using either analytic expressions or Monte‑Carlo simulations. A further mean‑field bias appears when the sky mask or anisotropic noise breaks statistical isotropy; it is removed by averaging the estimator over many noise‑only realizations. Iterative reconstruction schemes, where the lensed map is delensed and the estimator reapplied, reduce higher‑order biases and improve the fidelity of the recovered potential, especially at multipoles L ≳ 1000 where the signal‑to‑noise per mode is low.

Temperature‑only reconstruction is limited by the cosmic variance of the primary CMB and by foreground contamination (thermal Sunyaev‑Zel’dovich, point sources). Nevertheless, it provides a high‑signal‑to‑noise lensing map on arcminute scales, which is essential for delensing the large‑scale B‑mode polarization and for constraining the amplitude of matter fluctuations σ8 and the sum of neutrino masses.

CMB Lensing Reconstruction with Minimum‑Variance Temperature‑Polarization Estimators

When polarization data are available, the lensing signal can be extracted from multiple quadratic pairs: TT, TE, EE, TB, and EB. Each pair has a distinct weight function and a different noise bias NL(i). The minimum‑variance (MV) estimator is the inverse‑variance weighted sum of the individual unbiased estimators ϕ̂LM(i):

ϕ̂LMMV = ( Σi NL(i)−1 )−1 Σi NL(i)−1 ϕ̂LM(i).

The reconstruction noise of the MV estimator is then NLMV = ( Σi NL(i)−1 )−1. Because the TE and EE pairs probe the lensing‑induced E‑to‑B conversion, they add complementary information that is largely uncorrelated with the TT noise. In practice, the EB estimator is the cleanest at high L since the unlensed B‑mode power is negligible, but its noise is set by the instrumental polarization noise. The TE estimator helps break the degeneracy between the lensing potential and the primordial E‑mode amplitude.

Current generation experiments (Planck, ACTPol, SPTpol, Simons Observatory) implement the MV estimator in harmonic space using apodized masks and Monte‑Carlo debiasing of the N0 and N1 biases for each channel. The MV reconstruction reduces the lensing noise by 30–50 % relative to the temperature‑only case at L ∼ 100–1000, directly tightening constraints on σ8, the dark energy equation of state, and the neutrino mass sum. For future CMB‑S4, the MV estimator will approach the cosmic‑variance limit of the lensing potential up to L ≈ 3000, enabling percent‑level measurements of the growth rate fσ8 through cross‑correlations with galaxy surveys.

Quadratic Estimator Normalization
Derive the normalization factor AL for the temperature‑only quadratic estimator assuming an isotropic Gaussian unlensed CMB with power spectrum ClTT.
Start from the requirement ⟨ϕ̂LM⟩ = ϕLM. Insert the quadratic estimator definition and use Wick’s theorem for the Gaussian unlensed field: ⟨al1 m1 al2 m2⟩ = (−1)m1 δl1 l2 δm1 −m2 Cl1TT. The ensemble average of the quadratic term yields a sum over l of the weight function squared divided by the product of power spectra. Equating the result to ϕLM gives AL−1 = (2L+1)−1 Σl1 l2 (2l1+1)(2l2+1) (fl1 l2 LTT)2 / (Cl1TT Cl2TT). This is the standard normalization used in all temperature‑only lensing pipelines.
Exam‑Day Strategy
Remember that the MV estimator is not a simple sum of TT and EE estimators; it requires inverse‑variance weighting of each quadratic pair (TT, TE, EE, TB, EB). The normalization of each pair uses its own unlensed power spectra (TT, EE, TE). In multiple‑choice questions, the correct expression for the MV reconstruction noise is the harmonic sum of the inverse individual noises.

Neutrino Mass Effects on the CMB Lensing Potential

Massive neutrinos free‑stream out of gravitational potential wells below their comoving free‑streaming scale kfs ≈ 0.018 (mν/1 eV)1/2 Ωm1/2 h Mpc−1. This suppresses the linear matter power spectrum by ΔP/P ≈ −8 fν for k ≫ kfs, where fν = Ων/Ωm = Σmν / (93.14 eV Ωm h2). The lensing potential power spectrum CLϕϕ is a line‑of‑sight projection of the matter power spectrum, so the suppression imprints a scale‑dependent reduction in the lensing amplitude that grows with multipole L. For a total neutrino mass Σmν = 0.1 eV, the lensing power at L ≈ 1000 is lowered by roughly 3–4 % relative to the massless case.

CMB lensing measurements from Planck, ACT, and SPT break the geometric degeneracy between Σmν and the primordial amplitude As that plagues primary anisotropy analyses. The lensing likelihood adds a direct probe of the late‑time matter clustering, yielding a 95 % confidence upper bound Σmν < 0.12 eV when combined with baryon acoustic oscillation data. The constraint is driven by the high‑L tail of the lensing spectrum where the neutrino suppression is maximal. Future CMB‑S4 lensing will improve the sensitivity to Σmν ≈ 0.02 eV, reaching the minimal mass required by oscillation experiments for the normal hierarchy.

It is crucial to model the non‑linear matter power spectrum accurately when interpreting lensing data, because the neutrino suppression interacts with non‑linear growth. Emulators such as EuclidEmulator2 or Bacco incorporate massive neutrino effects and are used in modern Markov‑Chain Monte‑Carlo analyses. Ignoring the scale dependence of the free‑streaming scale leads to biased neutrino mass limits, especially when marginalizing over the intrinsic alignment amplitude in galaxy‑lensing cross‑correlations.

CMB Lensing Cross‑Correlation with the Cosmic Infrared Background

The cosmic infrared background (CIB) originates from dusty star‑forming galaxies peaking at redshifts z ∼ 1–3. Its angular fluctuations trace the same large‑scale structure that lenses the CMB, making the cross‑power spectrum CLϕ×CIB a powerful consistency test and a tool to calibrate the CIB bias. In the Limber approximation,

CLϕ×CIB = ∫ dχ Wϕ(χ) WCIB(χ) Pm(k = L/χ, χ),

where Wϕ(χ) = 3ΩmH02 (χ*−χ)χ / (2a χ*) is the CMB lensing kernel (χ* is the comoving distance to last scattering) and WCIB(χ) = a−1 dIν/dχ encodes the CIB emissivity history. The frequency dependence of the CIB allows tomographic slicing: higher frequencies (e.g., 857 GHz) weight lower redshifts, while lower frequencies (e.g., 217 GHz) probe higher redshifts, providing multiple cross‑spectra that break the degeneracy between the CIB bias and the matter power spectrum amplitude.

Planck measured the CIB‑lensing cross‑correlation at signal‑to‑noise ≈ 15, and ACT/SPT have pushed this to >30 by using higher‑resolution CIB maps from Herschel and SPT‑3G. The amplitude of the cross‑spectrum constrains the effective CIB bias bCIB and the product σ8Ωm0.5. Systematic uncertainties include Galactic dust leakage (which correlates with the CIB), shot noise from unresolved galaxies, and frequency decorrelation of the CIB signal across bands. These are mitigated by multi‑frequency component separation and by null tests using dust‑dominated frequencies.

The cross‑correlation also serves as a delensing channel: a template built from the CIB map can be used to subtract the lensing B‑mode contribution, complementing the internal CMB lensing reconstruction. Forecasts for CMB‑S4 combined with SPHEREx CIB maps predict a cross‑signal‑to‑noise >100, enabling percent‑level measurements of the growth rate fσ8 and a novel probe of the star‑formation history through the redshift evolution of the CIB kernel.

  • Confusing the temperature‑only quadratic estimator normalization with the polarization‑only one; the TT estimator uses the unlensed TT spectrum while the EE estimator uses the unlensed EE spectrum.
  • Assuming the minimum‑variance estimator is simply the sum of TT and EE estimators; it requires inverse‑variance weighting and inclusion of the TE cross‑term.
  • Neglecting the scale‑dependent neutrino free‑streaming scale when interpreting lensing suppression; the effect is not a simple overall amplitude shift.
  • Treating the CIB‑lensing cross‑correlation as a direct measurement of the matter power spectrum without accounting for the CIB bias and redshift kernel overlap.
  • Overlooking the mean‑field bias in quadratic estimators caused by anisotropic noise and masking; it must be subtracted using simulations.
  • Ignoring that the N0 bias for the temperature‑only estimator is larger than for the MV estimator, leading to overestimated reconstruction noise if not debiased.

Post-Born Corrections to CMB Lensing and the Lensing Bispectrum

The standard CMB lensing reconstruction assumes the Born approximation: photons travel along unperturbed geodesics and the lensing deflection is integrated along the background path. Post-Born corrections account for the fact that photons are deflected by intervening structure, so the actual trajectory deviates from the fiducial line of sight. This generates two main effects: lens-lens coupling, where the deflection at one plane depends on the deflection at earlier planes, and the geodesic deviation term, where the integration path itself is perturbed. Both effects are of order ϕ2 relative to the leading lensing signal and become non-negligible at high multipoles L ≳ 1000 for the lensing potential power spectrum CLϕϕ. The lens-lens term contributes a positive correction of ~1–2 % at L = 2000, while the geodesic term is negative and partially cancels it. The net post-Born shift in CLϕϕ is at the sub-percent level for L < 1000 but grows to ~0.5 % at L = 3000, which is comparable to the statistical precision of CMB-S4 and must be modeled to avoid biased cosmological parameters.

Beyond the power spectrum, post-Born corrections generate a non-zero lensing bispectrum BL1L2L3ϕϕϕ even for Gaussian initial conditions. This connected four-point function of the CMB arises because the lensing potential at different multipoles becomes correlated through the non-linear mapping. The bispectrum peaks in squeezed configurations where one multipole is much smaller than the other two, and its amplitude is directly sensitive to the non-linear matter power spectrum and the growth of structure. Measuring this bispectrum provides a consistency test of the lensing reconstruction pipeline and a probe of non-Gaussianity induced by non-linear evolution. Current analyses marginalize over post-Born uncertainties using simulations (e.g., LensPix, Planck lensing simulations) or include them as a fixed template in the likelihood. For future surveys, the post-Born bispectrum will be detectable at high significance and can be used to constrain the amplitude of matter fluctuations σ8 independently of the power spectrum, breaking degeneracies with baryonic feedback.

Baryonic Feedback Effects on the Matter Power Spectrum and CMB Lensing

Baryonic physics — radiative cooling, star formation, supernova feedback, and active galactic nucleus (AGN) feedback — redistributes matter within dark matter halos, suppressing the total matter power spectrum P(k) on small scales (k ≳ 1 h Mpc−1) relative to a dark-matter-only (DMO) simulation. The suppression can reach 10–30 % at k ~ 10 h Mpc−1 depending on the feedback model, with AGN feedback being the dominant driver at low redshift (z < 2) by ejecting gas from halo centers and reducing the central density. This directly impacts CMB lensing because the lensing potential power spectrum CLϕϕ is a line-of-sight projection of P(k). For L ≳ 1000, the lensing kernel receives significant contributions from k > 1 h Mpc−1, so baryonic suppression lowers CLϕϕ by up to 5 % at L = 3000. If unmodeled, this mimics a lower σ8 or a higher neutrino mass, biasing cosmological inference.

Modern analyses adopt two complementary strategies.
